Solving the World's Problems

Aldred Industries is solving the world's problems on paper, in public, one problem at a time. The research programme behind more than 2,000 published papers has turned from pure physics to the practical problems that kill and impoverish people: water that is not safe to drink, harvests that rot before they are eaten, homes that burn, flood, or shake apart, clinics with no power and no oxygen. Each problem is attacked with physics and engineering, computed from first principles, and published with a permanent DOI — whether the answer is a working design, an honest bound, or a public proof that a popular fix does not work. The catalogue below holds 832 papers, all published on November 10, 2026 — every one by William Alexander Aldred, every one free to read.

How the work is done

Every paper starts from the problem, not from a product. The computation is done from first principles and published with the paper, so anyone can check the arithmetic. Three kinds of result come out of the work, and each is published on its own merits: a design that works on the numbers; a bound that states honestly what cannot be done, and why; and a disproof — a published demonstration that a fix which is sold or believed does not survive the arithmetic. A disproof is a result, not a failure: it stops people spending scarce money on something that cannot work.

Every design is a computation, labelled SIMULATED, NOT MEASURED, until somebody builds it and measures it. Nothing on this page is described as built, tested in a home, or proven in the field. What is claimed is exactly what the papers claim: the physics, worked out in the open, with the limits printed on the face.

The catalogue

832 papers in forty-nine fields. Each entry states the headline result exactly as the paper reports it and links the paper's DOI; where the paper's own record states the problem in plain words, the entry states it too. Every paper is published open-access (CC BY 4.0) on Zenodo under the sole authorship of William Alexander Aldred (ORCID 0009-0000-1215-577X).

Water — 17 papers

Safe water where the grid and the supply chain do not reach: desalination priced honestly, leaks located, wells witnessed, and disinfection that needs no chemical deliveries.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24108541

A Solar-Powered Ultraviolet-C LED Disinfection Unit for a Village Standpipe, Sized on the Minimum Delivered Dose in Turbid Water

The problem. Safe drinking water where chlorine supply chains fail — disinfection with no consumable chemical, generated on site from sunlight, with its limits (no residual protection after treatment) stated plainly.

The result. Sizing a baffled serpentine UV-C reactor on its 5th-percentile (minimum-class) dose rather than its mean dose — the mean must be set at 2.01 times the required fluence, and a mean-sized reactor would pass 54.7% of its flow under-treated — a solar standpipe unit guaranteeing a 4-log bacteria-class dose at 5 NTU and 20 L/min needs 151.5 W of LED power, a 345 Wp array and a 404 Wh battery, while the virus-class dose costs ten times as much on every figure.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24108543

Locating Distribution Leaks by Pressure-Transient Timing on a Network of Low-Cost Loggers: A Method for Plastic-Pipe Networks Where Acoustic Correlation Fails

The problem. Cities and towns lose a large share of treated drinking water through leaks before it reaches a tap, worst where utilities are poorest, and the standard acoustic locating method fails on the plastic pipe those networks are made of.

The result. Timing low-frequency pressure transients between $30-class loggers at 250 m spacing and 10 Hz sampling locates leaks in polyethylene pipe to a standard error of 6.7 m — 4.07 times tighter than in ductile iron, because the plastic wave is slower — with a detection floor of a 3.08 mm equivalent hole at 250 m, covering a 5,000-person town's 50 km of mains with 201 loggers for $6,030 in hardware.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24108571

A Batch Reverse-Osmosis Cycle Whose Pressure Follows the Osmotic Curve: First-Principles Energy Accounting and a Sized Village Unit

The problem. Coastal and island communities with weak grids cannot afford the electricity that conventional seawater desalination demands, so abundant seawater stays undrinkable where power is scarcest.

The result. A batch seawater reverse-osmosis cycle whose feed pressure ramps to follow the osmotic curve of the concentrating charge uses 1.814 kWh per cubic metre of permeate at 35 g/L and 50% recovery — 21.7% less than a conventional continuous plant with isobaric energy recovery computed the same way (2.318 kWh/m3) — and needs no energy-recovery device.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24108587

A Psychrometric Bound on Atmospheric Water Harvesting: Where Air-Derived Water Can Work, Where It Cannot, and One Design That Respects the Bound

The problem. Households in drought regions are sold machines claiming litres of water from air at household power, with yields quoted without the temperature and humidity at which they were obtained; in the dry air where the need is greatest, those yields collapse to a fraction of a litre per kilowatt-hour.

The result. Below roughly 42% relative humidity, extracting water from air costs more energy — even for a thermodynamically perfect machine — than desalinating seawater and trucking it 100 km, and a realistic condensation machine beats a 500-km delivered supply only above 73.6–87.3% relative humidity, so harvesting is a humid-air, long-haul niche and not a water supply for arid air.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24108770

A Rain-Ingress Witness for Hand-Dug Wells and Boreholes: Mixing-Derived Detection Floors, Alarm Behaviour and Lead Time from a Low-Cost Conductivity and Temperature Logger

The problem. After storms, surface water enters wells and people drink the contamination before anyone knows; the witness flags the ingress before the next draw.

The result. A mixing-derived alarm (EC drop of 4 µS/cm sustained 2 h) on a low-cost conductivity/temperature logger fires at 0.81% ingress — 19.0 L in a 2,356 L hand-dug well, 2.85 L in a 353 L borehole — with no false alarms in the modelled 89-day baseline and 2.61 h of warning before the end of a 5%-in-6-h design storm.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24108806

A Village Chlorination System with No Chemical Supply Chain: Electrolytic Hypochlorite from Salt, Water and Sunlight, Sized End-to-End from Electrochemistry and Chlorine Decay

The problem. Disinfection that stops when a chlorine delivery does not come — a village water supply left unprotected because its disinfectant depends on a supply chain.

The result. A complete first-principles sizing shows a 10.0 m³/day village supply can be disinfected with no chlorine deliveries — a tank dose of 1.185 mg/L, decay-corrected production of 13.65 g of chlorine per day at 4.652 kWh/kg, requiring 63.5 Wh/day and 45.0 g of salt per day, with storage decay at 30 °C forcing a production oversize factor of 1.152.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24108834

A Batch Solar Water Pasteurizer with Counterflow Heat Recovery: Lethality Priced from Thermal Death Kinetics and Daily Output Sized from Insolation

The problem. Boiling drinking water where fuel is scarce costs scarce fuel or gathering time and fills homes with smoke, while the pathogens that matter are killed well below boiling if the water is held at temperature and its heat is recovered.

The result. With counterflow heat recovery at effectiveness 0.70, the batch cycle pasteurizes at 65 °C with a 10 min hold — a hold set by the enteric-virus class, which needs 9.0 min at 65 °C for a 6-log reduction while a 1 min hold gives it only 0.67 log — for a net 50.23 kJ per litre, 0.160 of the sensible heat of boiling, so one square metre of collector treats 179.2 litres per day and a 200 L/day school unit needs 1.12 m² of collector with a 50.0 L batch tank in 4 cycles per day.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24108838

Rainwater Tank Sizing by Stochastic Reliability Simulation: Storage–Reliability Design Curves for a Semi-Arid and a Monsoon Climate

The problem. A rainwater tank is often a poor household's largest single purchase, and folklore sizing leaves it too small to bridge the dry season or too large to ever fill.

The result. Storage–reliability curves from a Markov-generator daily balance (249 simulated years per configuration) give explicit tank sizes for 90% and 98% reliability in semi-arid (520.7 mm/yr) and monsoon (1511.5 mm/yr) climates; the semi-arid 40 m² / 50 L/day case is supply-limited (ratio 0.91) so no tank reaches 98%, the one-month rule delivers only 50.1–84.8%, and the largest dry-spell rule delivers 87.7–100.0% at up to four times the necessary size.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24109063

Freeze Desalination Priced Honestly: Full Accounting of Washing, Brine Entrainment and Refrigeration for Natural and Refrigerated Freezing of Seawater

The problem. Energy-poor cold coastal communities live beside undrinkable seawater and free winter cold, and are sold freezing as a cheap desalination route on the strength of its latent heat alone, without the washing, entrainment and refrigeration costs that decide whether it actually works for them.

The result. With brine entrainment and washing charged, refrigerated freeze desalination of seawater costs 53.2 kWh/m3 at COP 2.5 (17.7 times reverse osmosis at 3.0 kWh/m3), while natural tray freezing at −10 °C in light wind yields a net 13.25 L/m2/day — 75.5 m2 per m3/day — so the latent-heat advantage survives only where the cold is free.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24109065

Intermittent Water Supply: The Intrusion Volume per Outage Computed from First Principles, and a Household Break-Tank Design That Defeats It

The problem. Hundreds of millions of people receive piped water only a few hours a day, and when pressure dies the network draws contaminated soil water in through the same leaks that lose treated water when pressure is up, so the network doses itself between the treatment works and the tap.

The result. For the stated 10 km reference network, each supply outage draws 1.49 m³ of soil water in through the network's own leaks (2.98 m³/day, 0.30% of daily supply), and a nominal 75 L household break tank with a 40 mm air-gap inlet bridges the two-window supply pattern at 50 L/day essential demand with a chlorine re-dose interval of 6.0 days at 20 °C falling to 2.0 days at 35 °C.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24109067

The Electrodialysis–Reverse Osmosis Crossover for Brackish Groundwater: A First-Principles Energy Derivation and a Sized Village Electrodialysis Unit

The problem. Millions drink from brackish boreholes that are too salty for comfort yet too dilute for seawater-style reverse-osmosis habits, and the choice between electrodialysis and reverse osmosis for such water is usually made by habit rather than by calculation.

The result. Derived from stated first principles under one accounting rule, electrodialysis and reverse osmosis specific energies for brackish desalination to 0.5 g/L cross at a feed salinity of 6.90 g/L — ED lower below, RO lower above, and no crossover up to 20 g/L if RO brine energy recovery is removed — and a village ED unit for a 2.5 g/L borehole producing 5 m³/day needs 26 cell pairs at 10.50 A and 7.49 V, uses 1.82 kWh/day (0.41 kWp of photovoltaics at the stated yield), and is limited by gypsum solubility to a maximum recovery of 0.813.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24109077

The Virus-Removal Ceiling of Gravity Ceramic and "Nano" Point-of-Use Filters: A Sieving and Adsorption Accounting

The problem. Households in virus-risk settings spend money and trust on viral safety these filters cannot provide, and a filter believed to remove viruses displaces boiling and chlorination that actually inactivate them.

The result. At their stated pore ratings of 0.2–0.9 µm, gravity ceramic and "nano" point-of-use filters have a sieving ceiling of at most 0.66 log10 virus removal (0.000 log10 for 20–30 nm viruses), so marketed 4–6 log virus claims understate viral passage by factors of 2,163 to 216,290, while the same model confirms cyst removal at the 12-log cap and strong bacterial removal at finer ratings.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24109216

A Subsurface-Flow Constructed Wetland for a Household Cluster's Greywater, Sized by Removal Kinetics: The Bed Area, the Pathogen Reduction, and the Arid Salt Budget That Decide Its Reuse Class

The problem. Most of a household's water is greywater used once and discarded in places that cannot afford to use water twice, and most reuse guidance is a drawing rather than a computation, so it cannot say what its effluent may safely be used for or whether its salt slowly poisons the soil.

The result. A 12.92 m² subsurface-flow wetland sized by first-order kinetics for a 10-person cluster's 600 L/day of greywater reaches its BOD target and delivers a 1.68 log E. coli reduction sufficient for restricted irrigation, but it is 20.72 times the unrestricted-irrigation limit and barred from unrestricted reuse by a virus ceiling, while its arid salt loop closes for tolerant and moderately sensitive crops (leaching 2.96% and 10.12%) but not for sensitive crops (29.82%).

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24109218

The Reservoir Is the Sand: Extractable-Storage Accounting, Evaporation Suppression and Worked Sizing for a Sand Dam on a Seasonal River

The problem. Villages beside seasonal rivers watch a year's water pass in a few weeks of flow, then face months with a dry bed; a sand dam keeps some of that flow in the sand, but rule-of-thumb sizing never states whether it holds enough extractable water to bridge the dry season.

The result. Priced as a porous reservoir, a sand dam for 400 people at 20 L/day through a 180-day dry season needs 1,440 m³ of extractable water in a corrected sand body 147.4 m × 20 m × 2.0 m (5,895.8 m³ at specific yield 0.25), and storing in sand saves 2,995.6 m³ of evaporation against an open reservoir of the same plan area (34.0 m³ lost through a 0.30 m dry crust against 2,907.5 m³ open), equivalent to 359.2 days of village supply.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24109228

Defluoridation Priced at the Working Point: Activated Alumina Costed from Its Isotherm Against Nalgonda Precipitation Costed from Its Dose, and a 1,000 L/day Village Kiosk Sized on the Winner

The problem. Across the fluoride belts the only water is the well, its groundwater carries 2–10 mg/L of fluoride that cripples teeth and bones, and the standard treatment advice names a medium or a dosing method without pricing either at the chemistry of the water actually in the ground.

The result. Priced under one accounting rule with isotherm capacity at the working point and regeneration charged in full, adsorption on activated alumina costs USD 0.295, 0.864 and 1.735 per m³ at feeds of 2, 5 and 10 mg/L while Nalgonda precipitation costs USD 0.057, 0.229 and 0.516 per m³ but breaches the stated 0.2 mg/L residual-aluminium limit at the two higher feeds (0.389 and 0.875 mg/L), so precipitation wins only at 2 mg/L, adsorption wins at 5 and 10 mg/L, running unadjusted at pH 7.5 destroys 49.3–65.4% of alumina capacity, and a 1,000 L/day kiosk at the 5 mg/L design feed runs on two alternating 11 kg beds at USD 0.864 per m³ with 4.23 operator hours per week.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24109230

Designing a Fog Collector Instead of Copying One: Droplet-Capture Physics, Shade-Coefficient Optimisation and an Honest Seasonal Yield for an Arid Coastal Climate

The problem. Settlements on arid coasts where rain is rare live under fog that carries renewable water over them daily, but collectors are copied rather than designed, so mesh, geometry and expected yield are guesses instead of computed choices matched to the local fog.

The result. Computing droplet capture from the Stokes number across a stated fog spectrum selects a 0.3 mm fibre at shade coefficient 0.50 (total collection efficiency 0.3213), and a 40 m² panel of that design yields 111,041 L per season in the central arid-coastal fog class — 21.77 times the 5,100 L per year of a 40 m² rain roof at the same site.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24109346

Forward Osmosis Priced Honestly: A Regeneration Bound That Closes the Seawater Claim, and the Fertilizer-Draw Irrigation Design That Survives It

The problem. Irrigation water and desalination cost are both scarce in dry agricultural regions, and forward osmosis is marketed there as low-energy desalination although the draw regeneration that performs the actual separation is left out of the price.

The result. Regenerating any forward-osmosis draw costs more minimum work than desalinating the feed directly (bound 1.53 vs 1.06 kWh/m³ for seawater at 0.50 recovery; a stated NaCl draw totals 6.09 vs 3.04 kWh/m³ for RO at equal efficiency, and a thermolytic draw flips only if 56.49 kWh/m³ of heat at 60 °C is free), while a fertilizer-draw unit that skips regeneration entirely — 1,484 m² of membrane delivering 50 m³/day from 2.0 g/L brackish groundwater — over-delivers nitrogen 18.7–65.6× at full irrigation rate but, spread nitrogen-matched, is the complete fertilizer source for 32.8 ha of medium-demand crops while supplying 3.1% of their water.

Food and farming — 17 papers

Harvests lost after they are grown: drying, storage, cold chains without a grid, fertiliser recovered instead of bought, and water held in soil per dollar spent.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24108462

A Solar-Regenerated Desiccant Dryer for One Tonne of Smallholder Grain: Water Budget, Sizing and Failure Limits

The problem. Grain harvested wet in humid climates moulds within days, and farmers without fuel or grid power lose the harvest at the drying step.

The result. A solar-regenerated silica-gel dryer can dry one tonne of maize from 25% to 13% moisture in 3.0 days with 191.6 kg of gel and 24.0 m² of collector at 5.0 kWh/m²/day insolation, and it fails below 3.15 kWh/m²/day.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24108471

Farm-Scale Electrochemical Ammonia: A Costed Design and Its Failure at Small Scale

The problem. Fertiliser nitrogen comes from distant Haber–Bosch plants running on natural gas, so small farmers far from that supply pay ruinous prices or go without.

The result. Even with free electricity the designed farm-scale unit makes nitrogen at $16.30 per kg N, 8.8 times the delivered remote fertiliser price, so electrochemical ammonia is not economical at farm scale.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24108488

Oxygen-Scavenger Sachet Sizing for Hermetic Grain Storage: A First-Principles Oxygen Budget and Design Rule

The problem. A large share of harvested grain is eaten by insects or spoiled in storage before any person eats it, and smallholders under- or over-size the oxygen scavenging that makes hermetic bags work.

The result. At 25 °C a hermetic grain store needs 1,087 grams of iron-based oxygen scavenger per tonne upfront plus 14.2 grams per tonne for each month of storage to keep oxygen below the level that kills stored-grain insects for the whole storage period.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24108494

Non-Thermal Plasma Nitrogen Fixation at Village Scale: A Computed Design Budget for a Solar-Powered Gliding-Arc Unit, Compared with a Dielectric Barrier Discharge and with Delivered Synthetic Fertiliser

The problem. Fertiliser nitrogen comes from distant Haber-Bosch plants, and regions whose supply chains fail at planting time lose harvests their land could have grown.

The result. A solar gliding-arc unit captures 0.433 kg of nitrogen per day at USD 13.00 per kg — beating a dielectric barrier discharge by a factor of 17.8 in energy, but still costing 6.3 times more per kg of nitrogen than urea delivered to a remote region.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24108633

Soil Water Amendments Priced by Water Held per Dollar: A Common Yardstick, a Ranking Across Soil Textures, and the Break-Even Rainfall Deficit

The problem. Dryland farmers lose crops to short dry spells and are sold biochar, hydrogels, bentonite and compost as water-holding fixes with no common price tag to compare them by.

The result. On sand, bentonite and low-temperature wood biochar deliver the most plant-available water per dollar and are the only options that ever pay for a maize crop on water alone, while synthetic hydrogel at field rates holds only 0.10 mm of water and returns 222 times less water per dollar than the best option.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24108635

A Phase-Change Cold Box for the Last Mile Without a Grid: Computed Sizing, Hold Times, and Recharge for the +4 °C Cargo Class

The problem. Produce and vaccines spoil between the farm or clinic and the village because there is no refrigeration on the last leg; ice melts too fast and powered coolers need power that is not there.

The result. A 100 mm EPS box with 14 kg of sealed paraffin panels melting at 5.0 °C holds a 15.0 kg payload for 50.9 hours at 35 °C ambient in the model and recharges overnight in a freezer, while plain ice in the same box holds longer per kilogram but at 0 °C, below the safe cargo band.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24108663

An Evaporative Cooling Store for a Village Produce Market: Design, Simulated Performance Across Climates, and the Humidity Failure Line

The problem. Tomatoes, leafy greens and fruit rot within days in hot climates, so a market town without grid power throws away food it grew.

The result. A 3.0 cubic metre brick-and-charcoal evaporative store holds 24.6 degrees Celsius in 40 degree arid heat and extends modelled tomato life from 2.5 to 9.6 days for 6.7 litres of water per day, but it stops being useful above 61.5 percent ambient relative humidity at 35 degrees Celsius.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24108669

Edge-of-Field Runoff Capture Sized from First Principles: A Woodchip Denitrification Bioreactor for 20 Hectares of Drained Cropland

The problem. Fertiliser nitrogen washes off fields into rivers and wells — farmers lose nitrogen they paid for and downstream people drink it.

The result. A 612.0 m³ woodchip bioreactor on 0.255% of a 20 ha field removes a simulated 26.3% of the seasonal nitrate load and bypasses in rainfall events above about 26.5 mm in 24 h.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24108832

A Combined Solar Tunnel Dryer and Desiccant-Bead Store for Village Seed Saving: Design, Budgets, and Failure Floor

The problem. Farmers who save their own seed lose next year's crop in the bag — seed dried too slowly moulds, seed stored damp loses germination, and a failed planting cannot be undone.

The result. A 24 m² solar tunnel dryer held at or below 40 °C plus 198.6 kg of regenerable desiccant beads per tonne dries seed from 20 % to 9 % moisture in 1.56 days of dry-season sun, but the station fails below an insolation floor of 3.74 kWh m⁻² day⁻¹.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24108844

A Village Struvite Reactor Turning Source-Separated Urine into Fertiliser: Design and Phosphorus Budget for a 100-Person Community

The problem. Phosphorus is mined, shipped and priced as a strategic material while every village excretes it daily in urine and nearly all of it is wasted.

The result. A 200 L batch reactor for 100 people is calculated to recover 87.8% of urine phosphorus as 2.31 kg of struvite per person per year, and it stops paying on fertiliser value alone when magnesium oxide exceeds $1.23 per kg.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24108897

Capturing the Nitrogen That Escapes from Stored Manure: An Acid-Scrubbed Vent for a Covered Store, Its Nitrogen Budget and Its Break-Even Herd Size

The problem. A large share of manure nitrogen leaves the store as ammonia gas before it ever reaches a field — the farmer loses fertiliser he already owns, and the ammonia falls back as pollution.

The result. An acid-scrubbed vent on a covered store for an 800-cow dairy herd captures 6,919 of 7,358 kg of emitted nitrogen per year at $1.55 per kg N, and capture stops paying below 720 cows, below a mean slurry temperature of 11.3 °C, and above a vent rate of 0.76 air changes per hour.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24109017

Gravity-Fed Drip Irrigation from a Raised Tank Without a Pump: A Low-Head Design for a 0.1 Hectare Plot, Its Water Budget and Its Uniformity Limits

The problem. Small farmers on sloping or off-grid land water by hand or by flood because drip kits assume a pressurised supply that a village tank on a stand does not provide.

The result. A raised tank with no pump can drip-irrigate a 0.1 hectare tomato plot evenly (98.84% emission uniformity) on flat ground, delivering 6,644 L/day in four zones, provided uphill laterals stay at or below a 2.4% slope and 100 m in length.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24109023

A Village Solar Dehydrator for a 50 kg Mango Batch: Water Budget, Collector and Airflow Sizing, Drying Time and the Humid-Season Failure Line

The problem. When a crop ripens it all ripens at once — prices crash, the surplus rots, and the same village is hungry months later.

The result. A solar dehydrator with an 8.0 m² collector and a 110 m³/h fan dries a 50 kg mango batch in 1.96 days for a net advantage of $66.89 per batch, but in the humid season above 81.7% relative humidity it stretches past the three-day mould window and fails.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24109081

Rainwater Tank Sizing for Dry-Season Irrigation as a Computed Bound: Storage Is Set by Dry-Season Demand, Reliability Is Bought with Catchment Area

The problem. Dryland farmers get their year's rain in a few months and watch most of it run off, and a wrongly sized irrigation tank — empty by mid-dry-season if too small, never filling if too big — is an expensive failure.

The result. For a 100 m² dry-season vegetable field needing 78.2 m³ in an 800 mm rainfall climate, a four-in-five reliable tank holds about 76 m³ but only works if the catchment is at least about 1.5 times the field area, and the system stops closing at any tank size once rainfall variability exceeds CV ≈ 0.33 at that ratio.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24109097

Recovering Heat from Compost to Warm a Small Greenhouse: System Design, Heat Production Curve, and the Pile Mass Below Which the Plumbing Is Not Worth Installing

The problem. Market gardeners near cold seasons burn fuel they can barely afford to keep seedlings alive at night, while the compost heap a few metres away throws the same heat into the sky for free.

The result. An 8,000 kg compost pile with a water loop covers 46.0% of a 48 m² propagation greenhouse's 90-night heating demand (2,727 kWh delivered), and below a pile mass of 6,500 kg the plumbing is not worth installing.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24109114

A No-Electronics Witness Label for Sealed Grain Stores: Integrating Moisture Exposure and Seal Failure on One Passive Card

The problem. A farmer seals grain in a bag or drum and cannot tell, without opening it and breaking the seal, whether the store stayed dry and airtight or whether moisture crept in and the grain is quietly spoiling.

The result. A passive two-channel label costing US $0.057 per store at a volume of 100,000 reaches red in 0.76 days on a failed seal and in 0.66 days on grain sealed wet — the two cases each single channel alone misses in one direction or the other — while staying green through 90 days in an intact store, and it is honest only between 15 °C and 35 °C.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24109136

A Pond-and-Field Nutrient Loop, Closed by Arithmetic or Not at All: A Full Nitrogen and Phosphorus Budget for a 0.2 ha Fish Pond Serving 0.5 ha of Crops

The problem. Small farms buy fertiliser with cash they do not have while nutrients wash off their fields and out of their fish ponds unused.

The result. The pond loop supplies 57.7% of the crops' phosphorus demand but only 19.2% of their nitrogen, so the farm still buys $69.56 of fertiliser a year, and the loop turns polluting at 1,700 kg of feed a year.

Energy where people live — 17 papers

Power and heat for homes and villages the grid fails: storage that pays for itself, refrigeration without batteries, cooking without smoke, and the storage ideas that do not survive arithmetic, disproved in print.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24108419

A Village-Fabricable Chimney Rocket Stove for Clean Cooking: Design, Thermal Performance, Draft, and Indoor Exposure Estimates

The problem. Indoor smoke from open biomass cooking fires, which harms the health of cooks and children and consumes large amounts of gathered wood every day.

The result. The modelled stove cooks a stated day's food on 1.202 kg of wood instead of 3.505 kg and brings carbon monoxide below the guideline level used, but its fine-particle average remains 6.68 times the WHO 24-hour guideline.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24108436

Sand-Battery Thermal Storage for Home Heating: A First-Principles Residential Design for Three Sunless Days in a Cold Climate

The problem. Heating a home through cold, sunless days without high-cost batteries or high fuel bills.

The result. A 4.51-tonne sand store in 2.82 cubic metres holds enough heat for three sunless days in the stated cold-climate house, beats direct electric heating when charged with surplus solar and used often, but usually loses to a heat pump and cannot be filled from winter rooftop solar in the stated case.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24108475

Building-Scale Gravity Energy Storage for Off-Grid Homes: A First-Principles Design and Cost Test

The problem. Off-grid homes need overnight electricity storage that does not depend on short-lived, hard-to-replace chemical batteries in places with thin supply chains.

The result. Gravity storage fails at household scale — 235.8 tonnes at 10 metres delivers only 5 kWh overnight at about $1.96 per kWh versus $0.11 for lithium — and beats lead-acid only as a shared village hillside system of about 25 kWh per night or more.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24108531

Battery-Free Solar Refrigeration for Off-Grid Clinics and Homes: A Direct-Drive Design with Ice-Bank Thermal Storage

The problem. Clinics and homes without reliable electricity lose vaccines, medicines, and food when refrigeration fails, and the batteries that normally carry a solar refrigerator through the night are costly, short-lived, and hard to replace in remote places.

The problem. Homes and villages where lithium batteries are unaffordable or unavailable need electricity storage built only from abundant, locally obtainable materials.

The result. An iron-air battery loses to lithium iron phosphate for daily cycling at 10-hour duration because its round-trip efficiency is only 58.6 percent, but at 48-hour duration it delivers energy at $0.104 per kWh — about a quarter of the lithium cost — and it beats lead-acid at every duty tested.

The problem. Unreliable, short-lived battery storage for evening lighting and phone charging in off-grid villages and workshops, where a long-lived store built from air, steel and maintainable machinery would be valuable if it worked out cheaper.

The result. For evening light and phone charging for 50 households, village compressed-air storage returns only 15.9% of its charging electricity without heat storage (28.3% with it) and costs $1.545 per delivered kWh against $0.324 for lithium iron phosphate — and even with a free vessel the machinery alone costs more than a complete battery system — so it wins only where a tested vessel already exists, the charging power is truly surplus, and the air also does direct workshop work.

Counter-Rotating Vertical-Axis Wind Turbine Arrays: Designing Against the Spacing Tax from the Other Side

The problem. Wind power near where people live is taxed by spacing — large horizontal-axis machines must stand far apart, so generation sits far from homes, farms and workshops; packing small vertical-axis rotors tightly is the obvious counter-move, and this paper prices honestly what that move gains on a small patch of land, what it cannot gain at scale, and what it costs in money and upkeep.

The result. A specified array of small counter-rotating vertical-axis rotors (771.6 per hectare) produces a mean of 6.351–8.861 W per square metre of its own hectare across the full range of assumed neighbour-interaction factors — above the 0.5–1.0 W/m² horizontal-axis landscape baseline even when neighbours are assumed to hurt rather than help — but that advantage is borrowed from surrounding air and falls back to the same 0.5–1.0 W/m² bound at landscape scale, while costing 3.067 USD per rated watt and requiring 385.8 maintenance visits per hectare per year.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24109326

Wake-Steering Fleet Control for a Wind Farm: Designing Against the Spacing and Wake Tax

The problem. Wind farms lose a large share of their power to the wakes of their own upwind turbines and pay for the loss in extra land; this paper designs and prices the fleet-control countermeasure and states plainly where it stops paying.

The result. For a 20-turbine, 60 MW reference farm spaced 6 by 4 rotor diameters, an optimised wake-steering yaw schedule recovers +0.68% of fleet power in the dominant sector and +389 MWh a year (+0.25%, +64.3 W per hectare) in neutral air — rising to +4.37% in stable air, vanishing in turbulent air, and turning negative under fixed scheduling or direction spread, for a net of +$7,109 a year after added yaw maintenance.

For a 7.2 m by 4.8 m one-storey house in 140 mm stabilised-earth block with tie-columns at no more than 2.4 m spacing and a continuous ring beam, the computed design envelope ends at 0.291 g, set by in-plane shear of the short walls, with drift at that point of 0.0419% against a stated 0.40% limit.

The problem. It attacks cold homes for households that cannot afford heating fuel, by stating before construction what a zero-running-cost solar wall will actually deliver and where it stops being worth building.

Old age — 16 papers

Ageing at home, often alone: falls, cold rooms, failing senses, and the quiet witnesses that watch over a person who lives by themselves.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24110053

A Cold-Exposure Witness: Pricing the Dose a Cold Room Delivers to the Person in It

The problem. An older person living alone accumulates cold dose (temperature × hours, worst at night and when sedentary or asleep) in a house where nobody else is watching, so by the time a visiting relative notices the house is cold the exposure has already accumulated.

The result. In a stated 21-day cold snap, six of twelve dwelling/heating combinations cross the 150 °C·h concern line — low setpoint and one-room-only in Week 1 in all dwellings and leaky overnight-off on day 10 in Week 2 — and a three-day dose alert gives 1 to 7 days of warning before the crossing.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24110163

A Door-Exit Witness for Night Wandering: Detecting the Departure, Pricing the Minutes

The problem. A person with dementia living alone (or whose partner sleeps deeply) may leave the house at night in nightclothes into cold and traffic, with the danger growing every minute before anyone knows they have gone.

The result. At a 10-minute detection delay the modelled door witness gives 1.30 false alarms per month and starts a search 17.5 minutes after departure over 2.81 km² — 24.5 minutes before the stated 42-minute cold-exposure limit at −2 °C — while detecting only 58.8% of wandering departures under the stated assumptions.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24109852

Floor-Vibration Fall Detection for Older People Living Alone: A Detection Budget for a System with No Wearables and No Cameras

The problem. Falls at home are a leading cause of death and loss of independence for older people living alone, whose wearable pendants go unworn and who reject cameras.

The result. With a classifier that rejects 90% of everyday confusers, no floor-vibration configuration on timber or concrete meets both 0.80 hard-fall detection and a 0.01 false-alarms-per-day keep-on ceiling — only a 99%-rejecting classifier on a four-sensor timber floor qualifies (detection 0.819, 0.0071 false alarms/day), and soft falls are essentially undetectable (0.032) at that operating point.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24110079

A Hearing Aid from Phone-Grade Parts, Priced by Its Latency Budget

The problem. Proper hearing aids cost thousands and carry fitting queues and stigma, so an elder living alone with untreated hearing loss faces compounding isolation, missed alarms and missed conversation — and the obvious phone-and-earbuds substitute mostly fails because of delay and feedback, not price.

The result. Only a wired low-latency phone configuration with foam tips passes both the delay and gain tests for mild and moderate hearing loss (8.63 ms, 36 dB, $35.00 incrementally), while every wireless configuration modelled fails the own-voice delay limit on transport delay alone.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24109612

Impact-Attenuating Flooring Priced in Fracture Probability: A Model of Sideways Falls onto the Hip in Older Adults Living Alone

The problem. A sideways fall onto the hip causing a hip fracture in an older person living alone — an injury that often ends independent living at home and carries elevated mortality in the following year — where wearable hip protectors only work when they are actually worn.

The result. In the stated model, a foam-underlay floor in the bedroom and bathroom cuts the conditional fracture probability of a direct sideways hip impact for a 70 kg person from 72.9% on a rigid floor to 33.1%, at $3,154 per fracture averted over 15 years, but a two-room installation can address at most 55% of falls.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24109786

Hip-Protector Clothing Priced Honestly: Attenuation Times Adherence in Sideways Falls onto the Hip

The problem. Hip fracture from a sideways fall in an older person living alone — an injury that often ends independent living — where the cheapest proposed defence, padded hip-protector clothing, is frequently not worn because it is uncomfortable, awkward with incontinence, and forgotten on night-time bathroom trips when many falls happen.

The result. In the stated model, a 34 mm hip-protector pad cuts conditional fracture probability from 79.3% to 23.8% when worn, but at realistic fall-weighted adherence of 0.455 the population risk reduction is only 28.6% at $5,987 per fracture averted, and the protector needs 86.4% adherence to beat two-room flooring on cost per fracture averted while winning outright in the 45% of falls outside the treated rooms.

A Medication Adherence Witness: What an Opened Box Does and Does Not Prove

The result. A whole-box opening record makes a missed dose only 55.1% likely when no opening is seen and falsely reassures on 1.01 missed doses per month, while even compartment sensing plus a scale leaves a double-dose posterior of only 67.8% — the witness certifies openings, not ingestion.

The result. The One-Room Heat-Survival Pattern for an Elder in a Heat Wave: A Heat-Balance Model of the Cheapest Cooling Stack for One Survivable Room

The problem. Heat waves kill older people alone at home in overheating rooms — worst on top floors and in west-facing rooms — while air conditioning is exactly what a person on a small income does not have or cannot afford to run; this paper computes the cheapest ordered pattern for making one room survivable, and prints plainly where no passive pattern works.

The result. In a simulated top-floor, west-facing room, the dry heat-wave case is survivable with external shading, night purge, a fan and evaporative cooling at $1.22 per survivable-room-day, but humid heat and warm nights defeat every passive stack in the model (missing the 27 °C effective ceiling by 4.9 K and 6.9 K), so survival there costs a smallest-size room air-conditioner on top — $1.91 and $2.04 per survivable-room-day.

The One-Room Survivable Heating Pattern for an Elder in a Cold Snap

The problem. Older people on small incomes under-heat their homes in winter, and cold homes drive respiratory and cardiovascular illness and death, worst for a sedentary person living alone.

The result. At 0 °C outside, heating one room electrically to 18 °C costs £7.81/day against £8.13/day for a whole house held by gas at 16 °C (with the ranking flipping at +4.3 °C outside), but the unheated rest of the house reaches freezing when it is −2.7 °C outside, so the one-room pattern must be modified below that temperature.

The problem. Elderly people living alone depend on a daily check-in signal, and nobody prices the wait — act too fast and false alarms destroy trust in the system, wait too long and a person who fell at breakfast is found at supper.

The result. Under the stated assumptions, the cost-minimising escalation ladder for a missed 09:00 check-in is a soft prompt at 10:30, a telephone or neighbour check at 11:30, and entry at 12:30 — producing 1.17 expected false call-outs per year and discovering a real emergency on average 3.5 hours after the deadline, while no daily ladder can beat the 27.5-hour worst-case undiscovered interval the daily rhythm itself imposes.

The problem. Unattended cooking fires that begin when an older person living alone leaves a pan on the hob and cannot return to the kitchen — asleep, fallen, or incapacitated elsewhere.

The result. A $75 camera-free stove witness is computed to warn 5.21 to 13.24 minutes before the hazard point across four unattended-cooking scenarios, at a cost of 0.571 false alarms per week of normal cooking.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24109886

A Tap-Left-On and Slow-Leak Witness for an Elder Living Alone: A Single-Point Water-Flow Design with a Computed Detection Budget

The problem. A tap left running, a cistern that never stops filling, or a slow leak under a floor doing its damage silently in the home of an elder living alone who may not hear it or see it and may be away or incapacitated — where a flooded floor is also a fall hazard and a route to losing the home entirely.

The result. In the stated model, a single-point flow witness with a 30-minute day rule, a 15-minute night rule, and a 2-minute high-flow rule detects a basin tap left on at 4.00 L/min in 30.0 minutes by day (120.0 L lost, 108.0 L reaching flooring) and a burst pipe in 2.0 minutes (50.0 L) at 0.0005 simulated false alarms per month, but it can never catch any continuous leak below 0.40 L/min — a 0.15 L/min underfloor leak reaches 500 L in 2.31 days and 6,480 L in 30 days undetected.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24110139

The problem. An elder with failing sight who cannot afford a dedicated video magnifier (£320 / US$349) still has to read medicine labels, bills, letters, and cooker dials correctly, where a misread is a safety hazard rather than an inconvenience.

The result. A phone the reader already owns, plus a fixed stand and a 500 lux lamp (£35 / US$40 total), genuinely serves acuity 20/70 and 20/100 on medicine labels, newsprint, bills, and dials and serves 20/200 marginally for short checks, but field of view — not pixelation — limits it, so it cannot serve sustained reading at 20/400.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24113903

Freezing Food Without a Freezer: The Salt–Ice Mixture Bound — Lowest Temperature, and the Ice and Salt Consumed per Kilogram Frozen

The problem. People with ice but no electricity need to know how cold a salt–ice mixture can get food, and how much ice and salt each kilogram frozen will cost, before relying on it to preserve food.

The result. Sodium chloride mixtures reach at most −21.1 °C and calcium chloride about −51 °C (eutectic bounds); freezing 1 kg of food from 30 °C to −18 °C consumes 1.52 kg of ice plus 0.40 kg of NaCl or 0.36 kg of CaCl2, with NaCl holding only 3.1 K of margin at −18 °C, while CaCl2 at −40 °C consumes 3.67 kg of mixture per kg of food and crosses the stated practicality limit.

The problem. Builders of small structures need to know how deep to found footings and posts, and where a shallow foundation will be lifted by frost, before the damage is built in.

The result. The problem. People in cold regions who store winter-frozen food outdoors need to know how much insulation, around how much food, keeps it frozen through a warm spell with no power.

The result. To ride a 7-day spell at +8.0 °C from a −30.0 °C start under a hard limit of 0 °C with at most 10% melted, required polystyrene thickness falls from 0.232 m at 10 kg to 0.046 m at 100 kg and 0.027 m at 400 kg (mass exponent −0.461). The −12 °C high-quality limit is crossed after only about 1.4 days at design thickness, and a 25 mm wooden box at 100 kg reaches the hard limit in 0.94 days.

The problem. The dangerous advice that a person without fuel can meet their water needs by eating snow persists because nobody prices the melting energy the body must pay.

Weights and measures — 17 papers

Honest measure in the market: scales, balances, hidden moisture, and the quiet losses that bad measurement allows — and the checks that end them.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24113658

The Weight of the Bag Itself: Tare Bounds for Grain and Fertiliser Sacks Sold by Gross Weight

The result. When grain or fertiliser is sold by the sack and weighed gross — goods and sack together, with no deduction for the sack — the buyer pays the goods price for the packaging. This paper computes that tare fraction for stated sacks, dry and damp. A dry jute sack holding a nominal 50 kg of grain weighs 1.00 kg, a tare fraction of 1.96% of the gross weight; the same sack damp weighs 1.45 kg, a fraction of 2.82%. A 25 kg jute sack is worse in proportion: 2.34% dry and 3.36% damp. Woven polypropylene is far lighter, at 0.26% for a 50 kg fertiliser sack, and multiwall paper sits between, at 1.11% for 25 kg. At $280 per tonne for grain, the jute tare costs the buyer $0.28 per sack dry and $0.41 damp; a trader handling 2,000 sacks in a season passes $560 dry, or $812 damp, of sack weight charged as grain. The paper proposes a printed tare-marking rule — dry tare printed to the nearest 0.05 kg plus a stated damp allowance class — and computes its residual error: the systematic bias is removed and only a centred spread remains, at worst plus-or-minus 0.175 kg (0.35%) for the 50 kg jute sack, against a standing bias of 1.96% to 2.82% without marking.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24113652

The Short Tin: Fill Bounds for Standard Containers Sold as Full

The result. A container sold as full is usually filled to somewhere near the brim, and the buyer has no easy way to judge what a few millimetres of missing height are worth. The answer depends on where those millimetres are missing. The volume corresponding to a height shortfall is the cross-sectional area at the free surface multiplied by the shortfall, so the same 5 mm costs 307.9 mL in the wide body of a milk churn but only 39.3 mL in its narrow neck — a factor of 7.84 set purely by geometry. For a 1-litre oil tin, 5 mm short of the brim is 27.7 mL, or 2.78% of the contents; for a 5-litre tin it is 100.5 mL, or 2.00%. This paper computes shortfall tables for stated containers, derives a fill-line rule — place the legal fill line in a neck narrow enough that the permitted height tolerance cannot move the delivered volume by more than a stated fraction — and gives the maximum neck radius that satisfies the rule. At a 0.5% volume tolerance and a plus-or-minus 2 mm filling tolerance, a 1-litre container needs a neck diameter no wider than 5.64 cm.

The result. At standard 8.5% regain a 227 kg bale holds 17.78 kg water worth $31.30 of $399.52; humidifying to 18.0% gains $34.98/bale uncorrected, while one oven-dried sample detects a 1.568-point regain shift.

Ice Sold as Fish: The Glaze Fraction in Frozen Fish Sold by Weight

The result. Frozen fish is commonly protected by a thin coating of ice, called glaze. When the glazed product is weighed and charged at the fish price, the buyer pays the fish price for that ice. This paper computes, from geometry alone, how large that payment is. A fillet or piece is modelled as a rectangular slab, and a prawn or scallop as a sphere, coated uniformly with ice of stated thickness. Densities are taken as 917 kg/m³ for ice and 1,050 kg/m³ for fish flesh. For a cod fillet 180 × 80 × 20 mm, a 1.0 mm glaze is 10.90% of the total mass: at $18.00 per kilogram, $1.96 of each kilogram charged is ice, and the effective price of the fish itself rises to $20.20 per kilogram. The same 1.0 mm glaze is 19.33% of a small fish stick and 22.42% of a prawn of radius 10 mm, because small pieces have more surface per unit volume. A 2.0 mm glaze reaches 20.10% on the cod fillet and 38.87% on the prawn. The paper then specifies a thaw-and-drain test that a buyer or inspector can run with a kitchen or trade scale, and computes its own uncertainty: a 500 g sample on a 1 g scale resolves the glaze fraction to about ±1.6 percentage points in the worst case, while a 200 g sample on a 5 g scale resolves it only to about ±3.9 points, too coarsely to enforce a 5% limit. Glaze is legitimate protection. Charging for it as fish, without stating and subtracting it, is not a small rounding matter — at ordinary thicknesses it is a tenth to a fifth of the weight sold.

The result. Maize at 25.0% moisture vs 15.5% standard carries 112.4 kg excess water per tonne, worth $22.49/t and $611.60 per 27.2 t truckload at $200/t (11.24% of uncorrected payment); the transfer reverses below standard.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24113644

Heaped Against Level: The Volume Between a Heap and a Struck Measure in Grain Sold by the Container

The result. Grain is still sold in many markets by the container: a measure is filled, and the parties treat its contents as a fixed quantity. They are not fixed. A level, or struck, measure holds the cylinder volume. A heaped measure holds the cylinder plus a cone whose height is set by the grain's angle of repose. A shaken measure holds the same volume but more mass, because settling raises the packing density. This paper derives the heap volume in closed form, evaluates it for four measures and four grains, and prices the difference. For a medium measure (20 cm diameter, 15 cm deep, 4.712 litres struck), the heap adds 10.36% for wheat at a 25-degree angle of repose, 11.32% for maize, 16.15% for paddy rice, and 9.89% for soybean. Across all cases computed, the heap excess ranges from 7.42% to 19.81%. Settling adds a further 5% to 10% of mass at the same volume under the stated packing assumptions. Who gains therefore depends entirely on which filling convention a price was quoted under, and the swing is large enough — about $0.11 per medium measure of wheat for the heap alone, and about $1,053 per 10,000 such measures — that an unstated convention is not a rounding matter. The paper closes with a one-line convention rule that removes the ambiguity at no cost.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24113912

How Many Weighings Catch a Cheat? A Sampling Rule for Market Inspection at a Stated Short-Weight Rate

The result. A market inspector cannot test every stall every day. This paper computes, for a stated market of 120 stalls of which 10 (8.33%) use scales that give 30 g short on a 1,000 g purchase, how many check weighings are needed for two different jobs. For detection — finding at least one dishonest scale with 95% probability — the answer under purely random stall selection is 30 stalls checked once each, using the exact hypergeometric distribution rather than the cruder binomial approximation, which overstates the requirement as 35. Reliable risk information sharply reduces the work: if 6 of the 10 dishonest stalls sit in an identifiable group of 30 high-risk stalls, 11 checks inside that group alone reach 95%, and a mixed plan of 14 high-risk plus 14 other stalls reaches 99.32%. For estimation the picture reverses: pinning the market-wide short-weight rate to within ±2 percentage points at 95% confidence requires checking 104 of the 120 stalls, which is effectively a census, and the 30-stall detection sample estimates the rate only to within ±9.00 percentage points. The paper states plainly what neither rule can establish about any individual stall that passes or is never visited.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24113908

Timing the Piece Rate: Observer Error and Rounding Bounds on the Stopwatch Standard Behind Piecework Pay

The result. Piecework pay rests on a standard time: a worker is paid a fixed amount per piece, set by timing the job and converting that time at an hourly rate. This paper computes how errors in the timing propagate into pay. Three timing errors are modelled — stopwatch observer error, sampling variation between cycles, and rounding of the standard — under stated conditions: a single button-press standard deviation of 0.12 s, a systematic bias bound of 0.10 s per timed interval, and true cycle-to-cycle variation of 8% of cycle time. The central result is that the observer's hands are not the main problem. For a 30 second task timed over 10 cycles, observer error contributes only 0.18% while sampling contributes 2.53%, giving a combined random standard deviation of 2.54%, a 95% random bound of ±4.97%, and, with bias and rounding to the nearest 0.01 minute, a worst-case stated bound of 6.43% — about $1.16 per hour on an $18.00 hourly base. Rounding is not trivial either: rounding to the nearest 0.01 minute adds a standard deviation of 0.58% on a 30 second task, and systematically rounding up instead of to nearest donates a further 1.00%. A protocol that bounds the total error to a strict 2.0% at the 95% level requires about 91 cycles for a 30 second task with electronic timing to 0.1 s — far more than the handful of cycles commonly taken. A practical 30-cycle electronic protocol bounds the error to about 3.2%. Standards set from small samples should therefore be published with their bound, and pay disputes about a percent or two are, on these numbers, disputes inside the measurement noise unless many cycles were timed.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24113984

A Seal a Buyer Can Read: A Tamper-Evident Sealing Design for Market Scales, and the Attack It Cannot Stop

The result. A frangible serial label plus a doubled wire loop (computed breaking force 503 N) costs $0.94 per scale at quantity 100 and is buyer-readable in 16 s; it cannot stop whole-scale substitution with a copied serial ($19.30 costed), whose detection is 0% if the serial is never compared with the registry.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24113691

The Tilted Scale: How Far Off Level a Platform or Dial Scale Can Sit Before Its Indication Error Exceeds a Stated Fraction of the Load

The result. A scale that is not level weighs wrong, but by how much depends on its mechanism. For a spring or load-cell scale that responds to the force along its axis or normal to its platform, the indication is W·cos θ, so the error is second order in the tilt angle θ: it reaches 0.5% only at 5.73 degrees and 2% only at 11.48 degrees, tilts that are obvious to the eye. For a mechanism with a first-order term — an internal mass whose weight shifts the zero when the case tilts, modelled here by a zero-shift coefficient s in grams per degree — the thresholds are far stricter: at s = 5 g per degree, a 1000 g load reaches 0.5% error at 1.00 degree and 2% at 4.00 degrees. The practical question is therefore not "is it level?" but "which kind of scale is it, and what is its zero shift when tilted?" A buyer can answer the second part on the spot by tilting the unloaded scale and watching its zero. Two no-tools level tests are bounded here: a water surface in a 200 mm vessel, read to 1 mm end to end, certifies level to 0.29 degrees, and a rolling coin begins to move at roughly 0.29 degrees for a rolling-resistance coefficient of 0.005. Either test is more than fine enough for a cosine mechanism, and fine enough to keep a scale with s = 5 g per degree within about 1.4 g of zero shift.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24113893

Drift in a Working Spring Scale: Fatigue and Temperature Bounds Over a Season of Market Days

The result. A mechanical spring scale used at a market stall drifts for two distinct reasons: the spring softens as temperature rises and as it accumulates loading cycles, and permanent set shifts its zero. This paper combines both effects in a single stated model and computes the resulting error over a 180-day season of use. For a scale calibrated at 20 °C, making 200 weighings per day, the worst-case season-end error on a hot (35 °C) day is 29.1 g on a 2,000 g load (1.456%), 18.3 g on 1,000 g (1.832%), and 12.9 g on 500 g (2.583%), where the percentage is largest at small loads because the zero shift is a fixed number of grams. An unchecked scale crosses a 1% error at 2,000 g on day 47 in hot weather and on day 99 in typical (25 °C) weather; it does not reach 2% at 2,000 g within the season, or even within 1,000 days (1.861%), although it crosses 2% at 500 g on day 89 in hot weather. Keeping the error below 20 g at 2,000 g on the hottest days requires a check at least every 47 days; a weekly check holds the hot-day error at 2,000 g to 11.5 g (0.577%). A tighter target of 10 g at 2,000 g cannot be held for more than 4 days in hot weather, because temperature alone contributes 7.5 g at that load.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24113664

Checking a Spring Scale with Water: Error and Drift Bounds for a Buyer's One-Litre Test

The result. A buyer who suspects a market spring scale can carry a one-litre bottle of water and ask the scale to weigh it. This paper works out what that test can actually certify. Under stated, ordinary assumptions — a 1000 mL bottle with a ±15 mL volume tolerance, a ±5 mL fill or meniscus uncertainty, uncorrected water density, a tared empty bottle, a 20 g dial division, and ±15 g repeatability — a single one-kilogram test at 30 °C carries a worst-case uncertainty of 51.3 g (5.13%) and a root-sum-square uncertainty of 24.5 g (2.45%). The test therefore certainly detects a systematic short-weight bias only when that bias exceeds about 5%, and it cannot certainly detect biases of 1%, 2%, 3%, or even 5% under the worst-case bound. A 2% bias, invisible to this test, is worth $0.60 on a $30 purchase. Using five litres instead of one reduces the worst-case bound to 2.97% and the root-sum-square bound to 0.91%. The one-litre test is a coarse screen for gross error, not a certification of honest weighing at the one-to-two percent level.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24113680

The Unequal-Arm Balance: A Bound on Short Weight from Arm Asymmetry, and the Weighing Procedure That Cancels It

The result. An equal-arm balance delivers short weight if its arms are not equal, even when its weights are honest. Writing the two arms as L(1+e) and L(1−e), this paper shows that a single weighing errs by a fraction (1+e)/(1−e) − 1, approximately 2e: a half-percent asymmetry (e = 0.50%, a 3.0 mm arm difference on a 300 mm balance) already takes 10.1 g per kilogram, and stealing 20 g per kilogram requires an arm difference of only 5.94 mm on that balance. The paper then derives the double-weighing procedure — weigh once, swap goods and weights between the pans, weigh again, and take the geometric mean — which cancels the asymmetry exactly in the ideal model. The arithmetic mean, easier to compute in a market, leaves a second-order residual of only 0.20 g per kilogram at e = 1.00%. With 5 g weight steps and a ±2 g pivot deadband, the practical residual bound for the geometric mean is 2.26 g per kilogram root-sum-square (0.23%) and 3.20 g per kilogram worst case (0.32%). Any buyer who can insist on a swapped second weighing reduces an unknown arm error from percent level to a few grams per kilogram.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24113897

Wet Produce, Dry Price: Drainage Bounds for Vegetables Sold Straight from the Soak Tank

The result. Produce is often kept in or sprayed with water to keep it fresh and is sometimes weighed while surface water is still draining from it. The buyer then pays the produce price for water that runs off in the bag or at home. This paper computes that carryover from stated geometry. Smooth produce lifted from a soak tank is modelled with an initial surface film 0.20 mm thick, draining exponentially toward an adhered residual film of 0.03 mm. A tomato of radius 35 mm carries 1.77% of its weighed mass as surface water at lift-out, a cucumber 2.26%, and a bell pepper 2.44%. A lettuce head, with roughly three times the surface area of a smooth sphere of the same size and water trapped between leaves, carries 7.71%. On a 2.0 kg basket at an average $3.80 per kilogram, the water paid for is about $0.15 for smooth produce and about $0.23 if lettuce is half the basket. The paper then derives a drain-time rule — 120 seconds on a slatted rack, with one shake at 60 seconds, before weighing — and computes what it leaves behind: 0.27% for tomato, 0.35% for cucumber, 0.38% for pepper, and 2.16% for lettuce, falling to 1.11% for lettuce after 300 seconds. A two-minute rule therefore removes most of the unfairness for smooth produce at almost no cost, but leafy produce needs either a longer drain or a stated allowance.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24113964

A Witness Weight Set for the Price of a Drink: Water, Bottles, and Coins as a Buyer's Field Standard

The result. Mark-once bottles and a marked coin stack give worst-case bounds of +/-0.90% at 100 g, +/-1.218% at 499.1 g, and +/-0.918% at 998.2 g (0.577% with temperature correction); an unmarked circulated coin stack is bounded only at +/-4.40% and is not a standard.

Carried Against the Body: A Heat-Balance Bound for Cloth-Wrap Carrying by Climate

The problem. Infants carried in cloth wraps in hot climates store heat on a timescale of tens of minutes, with no widely available statement of safe carrying durations and rest intervals by climate.

The result. For a walking caregiver carrying a 7 kg infant in front at 70% humidity, the computed maximum continuous carrying duration to a stated 1.0 K heat-storage margin falls from 59 minutes at 25 °C to 29 minutes at 30 °C, 19 minutes at 35 °C, and 14 minutes at 40 °C; at 70% humidity and 35 °C or above, unwrapped rest in shade cannot remove stored heat at all in the model, so no carrying duration is safe without cooler air or active cooling.

The result. The problem. Preparing formula with water hot enough to inactivate the organisms powdered formula may carry costs scarce fuel at every feed, and stored boiled water silently leaves the safe temperature band.

The result. The problem. The assumption that small home formula-mixing errors are harmless, when biased errors systematically deliver over-concentrated feeds to infants whose kidneys and sodium balance tolerate little error.

The result. The problem. A widely repeated claim — that a baby always needs a hat because most heat is lost through the head — drives dressing practice without its conditions ever being stated or tested.

The result. The result. The result. Scheduling a hand-watered plot by the middle of the pan-coefficient band (0.70, band 0.60–0.80) leaves the crop 11.25 stress days in a 90-day season whenever the true coefficient sits at the top of the band. The originated rule — water when cumulative pan readings reach a tabulated trigger (32.61 mm for loam at mid-season) and apply 0.875 of the readily available water (26.25 mm net, 30.88 mm gross on loam) — holds stress at zero across the whole band on sand, loam, and clay, at a worst cost of 3.75 mm drainage per watering on loam and about two extra waterings per season.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24114365

How Far Does One Buried Clay Pot Really Reach? A Wetted-Volume Bound for Pitcher Irrigation, and a Test of the Coverage Claims Made for It

The result. A 15-litre buried clay pot seeping 1.0 L/day wets a sphere of radius only 0.241 m in loam after 7 days (0.391 m after 30 days) and sustainably serves just 0.2 m² at 5.0 mm/day crop demand — so the circulating claims that one pot serves 4 m², six plants at 0.5 m spacing, or a 1 m × 1 m bed fail by factors of 20, 7.5, and 5.0 respectively; what survives is one plant planted against the pot (two within 0.3 m only at the top of the seepage range), with a 12.0-day refill interval.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24114727

The Habituation Bound: How Long a Static Bird Scarer Actually Protects a Ripening Crop, and Why No Rotation of Static Scarers Holds Deterrence for a Full Ripening Window

The result. Under a stated habituation model (effectiveness 0.85 at installation, decaying with a 3-day time constant towards a 0.05 floor), a static scarer on sweetcorn stays above a 0.50 protection threshold for only 1.73 of a 21-day ripening window and still loses 43.84 percent of the crop against 52.5 percent unprotected; changing to a different scarer type every 1.25 days is the best rotation found and still loses 22.35 percent, and no rotation of static scarers holds the threshold for the whole window.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24114481

The Dry-Enough Bound: Hours, Nights, and Mould Risk in Sun-Drying Maize Seed to Safe Storage Moisture

The result. Sun-drying maize seed from 20% to a safe 12% moisture takes 2.65 hours in a thin layer at 40% humidity but 8.11 hours in a 5 cm layer and 14.60 hours at 8 cm, and it cannot reach the target at all when daytime humidity exceeds 59.1%, because equilibrium moisture then sits above the target; one uncovered night returns 4.52 moisture points to finished seed against 0.33 points for a night indoors, and deep layers accumulate up to 5.6 hours in the stated mould-risk condition against 1.1 hours for a thin layer finished on the first morning.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24114299

A Viability Bound for Farm-Saved Maize Seed: Months Above Sowing Quality for Five Storage Methods, and the Moisture or Temperature Each Method Would Need

The result. Applying Harrington's rules of thumb in a probit viability model to maize starting at 96% germination, an open gourd or basket in a hot house stays above 85% germination for only 0.86 months, ash-covered seed for 4.85 months, a sealed tin for 8.32 months, a sealed bottle in a cool pit for 55.10 months, and a clay pot with desiccant for 60.93 months; a twelve-month life at sowing quality requires seed held at roughly 10% moisture or below at house temperatures (9.77% for the basket's temperatures, 10.24% for the tin).

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24114622

The Shade Bound: Where Shade Cloth Stops Paying for Lettuce in Hot Climates

The result. For lettuce under stated assumptions, shade pays only where severe heat and surplus light coincide: in a hot, bright season (DLI 45 mol/m2/d, peak air 38 °C) yield peaks at 0.463 at 40% shade versus 0.409 unshaded, while 70% shade falls back to 0.414; in a hot cloudy season the optimum is about 25%, and in mild or cool seasons no shade fraction pays, with 70% shade costing 26.8% of yield at peak air 28 °C.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24114675

The result. Under the stated reciprocal-yield relation for headed lettuce (1/w = 3.33 + 0.20N under standard water), total yield per square metre rises at every density toward a 5.00 kg ceiling that takes 316.3 plants per square metre to approach within 5 percent, but marketable yield at a 0.12 kg grade peaks at 25 plants per square metre (3.001 kg per square metre), giving a spacing rule of 20.0 cm under standard water, 24.5 cm when dry, and 17.3 cm when irrigated; sowing at 100 plants per square metre wastes an extra USD 1.67 per square metre of seed on heads averaging 0.043 kg.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24114642

The Windbreak Bound: When a Shelter Strip Returns More Than the Land It Takes

The result. For a stated 5 m barrier costing an effective 4.25 m of field length, break-even is set mainly by porosity and wind climate, not field size: a dense barrier (porosity 0.2, shelter to 118 m) pays once damaging days exceed about 17% of the season on a 100 m field and gains 3.9% of yield at a damaging-day fraction of 0.30, a porosity-0.5 barrier needs about 29% damaging days, and a porosity-0.8 barrier needs an unrealistic 63–65% and loses yield at every field size computed.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24114522

Walking a Contour with an A-Frame Level: How Far the Line Drifts After a Hundred Stations

The result. After 100 stations with a 2.0 m A-frame (200 m of hillside), the walked contour drifts by a design bound of 208.59 mm from the true contour — 111.11 mm of steady bias from a 0.5 mm crossbar mark offset plus a random walk of 48.74 mm standard deviation — displacing the line 2.086 m sideways on a cross-slope gradient of 0.10; the bias over a fixed width is independent of frame size, the random term is minimised near a 1.0 m span, and holding a 50 mm tolerance requires checks against a known level every 13 stations (26 m), or every 26 stations if the frame is reversed at every station, since no unchecked 200 m walk meets the tolerance at any span (best bound 195.67 mm).

Rounded Ages in a Written Register: A Digit-Preference Bound on the Error in Mean Age and Dependency Counts

The result. In a stated model register of 100,000 people (true mean age 32.7351 years), heaping a fraction of ages onto the nearest multiple of five drives the Whipple-style index from 99.98 to 259.99 at 40 percent heaping and 420.00 at 80 percent, yet mean age errs by only −0.0582 years even at 80 percent heaping, while the dependency counts err in a fixed direction (at 40 percent heaping: under-15 share −1.336 points, 65-and-over share +0.628 points, dependency ratio −2.101 points). How Much Livestock Loss Can Hide in a Register: The Audit Interval That Bounds It

The result. Under stated assumptions (counting error the larger of 2 percent of the herd and 3 animals, register corrected to the count at each audit), the largest loss that can pass one audit is twice the counting error — 20 animals in a 500-head herd, but 6 animals, 30.0 percent of the herd, in a 20-head herd. Because the slack renews at each reconciliation, a steady loss of 4.0 percent of the herd per year passes annual audits of a 500-head herd indefinitely (16.0 percent under quarterly audits); holding a steady hidden loss to 2 percent per year requires counting accurate to 1 percent at annual audits, and a 20-head herd cannot meet the bound at any interval shorter than 15.0 years, so register-keeping alone cannot protect a very small herd. A Monte Carlo check found a steady loss at the 2 percent bound in a 500-head herd is detected at only 50.59 percent of annual audits. SIMULATED, NOT MEASURED.

The result. How Complete Is the Village Register? A Two-List Estimate with Its Uncertainty, and When the Second List Is Worth Collecting

The result. Using the Chapman two-list estimator on stated planning counts, a register holding 240 of a true 300 events, checked against an equally complete second list with an overlap of 192, yields an estimated total of 299.94 and a completeness of 80.02 percent with a 95 percent interval of 77.8 to 82.3 percent (half-width 2.25 points). The derived decision rule requires a second list large enough to produce an expected overlap of roughly 60 to 160 events (for example 135 events against a register of 240 at a total of 300) for a ±5-point estimate; below an overlap of about 20 the interval for the total is too wide to quote as a percentage (upper-to-lower ratio 3.948 at an overlap of 5), and an overlap inflated by only 10 percent through dependence between the lists overstates completeness by 7.92 points. Squaring a Field with a Knotted Rope: Corner Error, Area Error, and What the Diagonal Check Can and Cannot Catch

The result. For a nominal 40 m by 30 m field laid out with a 3-4-5 knotted rope, the corner angle has a root-mean-square error of 0.404 degrees and side-length errors produce a typical area error of 0.5657 percent, while the traditional diagonal check — though it bounds corner error to 0.1194 degrees at a 0.10 m tolerance — is exactly blind to isosceles trapezoids, which pass with equal diagonals at area shortfalls of 2.02 percent (12 m total base difference) and up to 10 percent; adding a diagonal bisection check within 0.15 m, sides held to 0.3 percent, diagonals agreeing within 0.25 m, and knots placed within 18.08 mm guarantees a worst-case area error of 0.6055 percent.

How Much Too Large a Sloping Field Measures: The Slope Overstatement Bound and the Correction Rule

The result. Ground measurement overstates horizontal area by 1.5427% at 10 degrees, 3.5276% at 15 degrees, and 6.4178% at 20 degrees, crossing 2% at 11.3649 degrees and 5% at 17.7528 degrees; a field half flat and half at 20 degrees overstates by 3.2089%, more than double the 1.5427% a mean-slope correction would remove, while rope sag at a ratio of 0.02 adds only 0.2134%, so the correction rule is patch-wise division by the secant of each patch slope estimated by rope and plumb line over a 10 m run.

Reading a River to the Nearest Centimetre: A Stilling-Tube Staff Gauge and the Averaging Rule That Certifies It

The result. Direct reading certifies a level within plus or minus 1 cm at 95 percent confidence with 49 readings spaced at least 4 s apart against wind waves of 0.05 m amplitude and 8 s period, but needs 193 readings over 32.0 minutes against a 0.10 m, 20 s surge and is impractical there; a stilling tube of 200 mm diameter meets both a 1 cm residual and a 2 cm lag target at a 2 m per hour rise only with a hole between 11.31 mm and 12.06 mm (a nominal 12 mm hole: time constant 31.99 s, residual 0.99 cm, lag 1.777 cm), and at a 3 m per hour rise no hole in any tube size meets both targets, since damping requires a time constant of at least 31.67 s while the lag target allows at most 24.0 s.

The Split Tally as a Cooperative Record: What Two Halves of a Stick Prove, and the Discrepancy Rule That Makes Them Useful

The result. A zoned split tally on a 300 mm stick represents up to 599.5 kg at 0.5 kg resolution, exposes any one-sided notch addition with certainty when the halves are compared, and cuts expected zone-miscount settlements from 5.97 to 0.6 in 400 when counted fitted together; the proposed 1 kg discrepancy tolerance can hide at most 400 kg per year over 400 settlements, or 1.67 percent of a 24000 kg throughput, in the worst case.

Fishing and small boats — 17 papers

Small boats, traps, nets and the shore: safe loads, gear that fishes as intended, and fish kept sound from the water to the market.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24115024

A Salt-Penetration Rule for Whole and Split Fish: Salting Times by Thickness, the Spoilage Thickness Limit, and Dry Salting Compared with Brining

The result. Salt preserves fish only where the salt has actually arrived, and the last place it reaches is the centre of the thickest part. This paper models salt uptake as one-dimensional diffusion into a slab of fish flesh, with an effective diffusivity of 3.0e-10 m2/s as a stated assumption, and computes the time for the centre to reach 10 percent salt in the water phase, a stated keeping target. Dry salting, with a saturated surface film at 26 percent, and brining in a stated 15 percent brine are computed as separate cases throughout. Salting time grows with the square of the half-thickness, so splitting a fish open, which halves its thickness, cuts the curing time by a factor of 4.0 exactly in this model. Against a stated spoilage clock of 36 hours for an unsalted centre at 25 degrees C, a whole fish thicker than 2.30 cm dry salted, or 1.69 cm brined, has a centre that spoils before the salt arrives; splitting raises those limits to 4.60 cm and 3.38 cm of original thickness. A 4 cm whole fish needs 109.0 hours dry salted and 201.2 hours in brine, against 27.2 hours dry salted once split. Brining at 15 percent takes 1.846 times as long as dry salting to reach the same centre target, because its surface concentration is lower. The study is simulated, not measured, and its limits are upper bounds on safe practice, for reasons set out in the Limitations.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24115039

A Waiting Rule for Newly Anchored Fish Aggregating Devices: How Long a New Device Must Stand Before Fishing It, with a Mooring Scope and Anchor Table

The result. A fish aggregating device, a moored raft or buoy under which fish gather, does not fish at full strength on the day it is anchored. This paper models the build-up of the aggregation as an approach to a settled level with a time constant of 7 days, a stated assumption informed qualitatively by published accounts of aggregation behaviour, and turns that curve into a waiting rule. A new device reaches 50 percent of its settled catch rate after 4.85 days, 80 percent after 11.27 days, 90 percent after 16.12 days, and 95 percent after 20.97 days. The recommended rule is therefore to let a new device stand for about 16 days before fishing it in earnest if 90 percent of the settled rate is wanted, or about 21 days for 95 percent. Fishing every day from day 1 at a settled rate of 40 kg per day, taken as a stated assumption, returns a mean of only 41.2 percent of the settled rate across the first week and a cumulative shortfall of 22.8 percent across the first 28 days, not because waiting creates fish, but because early effort is spent at low rates. Alongside the waiting rule, the paper originates a mooring scope rule in which the required scope ratio grows with surface current, from 1.312 at 0.5 knots to 1.95 at 2.0 knots, and tabulates rode length and concrete anchor mass by depth and current: at 200 m depth and 1.0 knots the rode is 300.0 m and the computed minimum concrete anchor is 187.3 kg in air, rising to 915.9 kg at 2.0 knots. The study is simulated, not measured, and the anchor figures are steady-current minima that exclude wave and storm loading.
