An Ice-Planning Rule for Small Fishing Trips: Ice Needed to Chill the Catch and to Offset Heat Leak, and the Trip Length at Which a 1:2 Ice-to-Fish Ratio Runs Out

The result. How much ice should a small fishing boat carry? Guidance is usually given as a single ratio of ice to fish, with no account taken of how warm the day is or how long the trip lasts. This paper separates the two jobs ice has to do — chilling the catch from its loading temperature to about 0 °C, and replacing the ice melted by heat leaking through the box walls during the trip — and combines them into a single planning rule for a reference insulated box (50 mm expanded polystyrene walls, 100 kg of fish). The rule is R = 0.01078 × T + 0.00706 × T × t, where R is kilograms of ice per kilogram of fish, T is ambient temperature in °C and t is trip length in days. Chilling alone consumes 0.216 kg of ice per kilogram of fish at 20 °C and 0.377 kg/kg at 35 °C. Heat leak melts a further 14.12 kg of ice per day per 100 kg of fish at 20 °C, rising to 24.72 kg per day at 35 °C. A commonly carried ratio of 1:2 ice to fish (0.50 kg/kg) is therefore exhausted after 48.3 hours at 20 °C, 20.0 hours at 30 °C and only 11.9 hours at 35 °C; indeed, at 35 °C a trip of just 12 hours already requires 0.501 kg/kg, slightly more than the whole 1:2 allowance. A two-day trip at 30 °C requires 0.747 kg/kg, and a five-day trip at 35 °C requires 1.613 kg/kg. A fixed ratio cannot be right at all temperatures and trip lengths; the rule and table given here replace it with arithmetic a skipper can do before loading. The study is simulated, not measured.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24115070

Sizing the Walls of a Low-Cost Insulated Fish Box: Expanded Polystyrene versus a Sawdust-Filled Double Wooden Wall

The result. An insulated fish box is usually built with whatever sheet insulation is at hand, at whatever thickness fits, and its performance is discovered on the first hot trip. This paper instead sizes the walls from a stated duty: a box of fixed external size 1.00 m × 0.60 m × 0.60 m must hold 40 kg of fish, loaded at 5 °C, with a 25 kg ice charge, at or below 4 °C for 48 hours at an ambient temperature of 35 °C. Because the contents remain at about 0 °C while any ice remains, the duty reduces to one condition — the ice must not be exhausted — and the wall thickness follows from a conduction balance. Two low-cost constructions are sized. Expanded polystyrene sheet (thermal conductivity 0.035 W/(m·K)) requires a wall 69.5 mm thick, which in practice means building to 70 mm; it leaves an internal volume of 182.9 litres, 50.8 percent of the 360-litre gross volume. A double wooden box with dry sawdust fill (0.09 W/(m·K)) requires 136.2 mm, built to 140 mm, and leaves only 78.1 litres — 21.7 percent of gross — which is barely more than the 76.5 litres the fish and ice themselves occupy, a spare volume of just 1.6 litres. If the fish are loaded warm at 30 °C instead of 5 °C, the sawdust design ceases to be workable inside the same envelope: it would need 190.5 mm walls and its 29.7-litre interior could not hold the load. The paper gives the sizing rule, a thickness table across trip lengths and temperatures, and the volume each wall costs, so a builder can choose with the trade-off in front of them. The study is simulated, not measured.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24115080

A No-Ice Working Clock for Landed Fish: Hours to the Quality Limit across Temperatures, and the Extension from Wet Sacking in Shade

The result. Where ice is unavailable, unaffordable, or exhausted, a seller's practical question is simply: how long have I got? This paper builds a working clock for that question. Taking a reference shelf life on ice of 12 days (288 hours) at 0 °C to the limit of acceptable quality, and a Q10 of 2.5 for the temperature dependence of spoilage, the remaining saleable time is 182.1 hours at 5 °C, 115.2 hours at 10 °C, 72.9 hours at 15 °C, 46.1 hours at 20 °C, 29.1 hours at 25 °C, 18.4 hours at 30 °C and 11.7 hours at 35 °C. The clock therefore runs about 15.62 times faster than on ice at 30 °C and 24.71 times faster at 35 °C: a catch landed in the morning in hot weather reaches the quality limit the same evening. The paper then evaluates the oldest no-ice remedy, covering the boxes with wet sacking in shade so that evaporation holds the fish near the wet-bulb temperature. The benefit depends entirely on humidity. In arid air at 35 °C with a wet-bulb depression of 9 °C, the window lengthens from 11.7 to 26.6 hours, an extension of 14.9 hours. In temperate summer air at 24 °C with a depression of 4 °C, it lengthens from 31.9 to 46.1 hours, an extension of 14.1 hours. In humid tropical air at 32 °C with a depression of only 2 °C, it lengthens from 15.3 to 18.4 hours — an extension of just 3.1 hours, helpful for reaching a market but not for holding a catch overnight. Wet sacking is worth doing in dry air and is a weak defence exactly where heat and humidity combine. The study is simulated, not measured.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24115099

The Escape Length of a Stretched Mesh: An Inscribed-Circle Rule Linking Mesh Size, Opening Shape, and Fish Girth

The result. Fishermen and gear designers routinely need to answer a plain question: given a netting mesh of a stated size, what is the largest fish that will still get through it, and what is the smallest fish that will be held? This paper originates a simple geometric rule for that question. A mesh is treated as a rhombus of four bars, and the widest fish cross-section that can pass is identified with the circle inscribed in the opening, whose diameter is the altitude of the rhombus. Combining that diameter with a stated girth-to-length relation for a fusiform fish (maximum girth equal to half the length) and a stated allowance for body compression gives two thresholds for every mesh: a nominal escape length and a larger escape length that a fish can reach by compressing its girth by one tenth; fish longer than the second value are reliably retained. For a 100 mm stretched mesh the rule gives an escape length of 335.1 mm for a square opening and 288.3 mm for a diamond opening, a ratio of 0.86, so opening shape alone changes the escaping size by about one seventh at fixed mesh size. Across stretched mesh sizes from 25 mm to 150 mm the escape length rises from 73.3 mm to 509.6 mm for square openings and from 61.6 mm to 439.5 mm for diamond openings. The full table is provided so that a mesh can be chosen directly from the length of fish to be released or held. The study is simulated, not measured.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24115105

A Safe-Load Rule for Dugout Canoes: Freeboard, Metacentric Height and the Cost of Standing Up

The result. A dugout canoe is usually loaded by eye: fish, gear and people are added until the boat feels low or tender, and the judgement is made afloat, when changing it is hardest. This paper replaces that judgement, for planning purposes, with a computed rule. A simple hydrostatic model of a dugout hull — displacement from a block coefficient, freeboard from hull depth minus draft, and transverse stability from the metacentric height — is applied to four canoe sizes, and the largest payload is found at which two stated limits both hold: a minimum freeboard of 0.15 m and a minimum metacentric height of 0.15 m. In every size the stability limit is reached long before the freeboard limit. For the reference canoe of 5.0 m length, 0.85 m beam and 0.45 m depth, the safe payload with everyone seated is 300 kg, while the freeboard alone would allow 588 kg; loading to the freeboard figure would leave the metacentric height negative in the standing case and at the stated minimum in the seated case. When the payload is treated as standing, the safe payload of the same canoe falls to 151 kg, a reduction of 149 kg or 49.7 percent. Across the four sizes the reduction on standing ranges from 47.5 percent to 55.9 percent. The practical rule is therefore twofold: load a dugout by its stability figure, not by the space or freeboard that remains, and treat standing up in a loaded canoe as roughly halving the load the canoe can safely carry. The study is simulated, not measured.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24115127

Hanging Ratio Moves a Gillnet's Catch Band but Cannot Narrow It: A Geometric Selection Rule

The result. Gillnetters often speak of the hanging ratio — the length a net is hung to, divided by its stretched length — as a way of tuning which fish the net takes, and it is natural to hope that slackening or tightening the hang might narrow the range of sizes caught around a target length. This paper tests that hope with a geometric model. The mesh opening at a given hanging ratio is a rhombus whose width for a fish's body is set by its altitude; a fish is caught when the mesh can be pushed over its gills but cannot pass over its greatest girth. Both edges of the caught-length band turn out to be proportional to the same quantity, so their ratio is fixed at 1.4238 for every hanging ratio from 0.30 to 0.70: hanging ratio moves the band and stretches it, but cannot change its relative width. The result is then worked in the form a fisherman actually needs. On a fixed 100 mm mesh, raising the hanging ratio from 0.30 to 0.70 moves the band from 172.9–246.2 mm to 302.0–430.0 mm. If instead the mesh is re-chosen at each hanging ratio so the band stays centred on a 300 mm target, the band is 247.5–352.5 mm at every hanging ratio — a width of 104.9 mm that no choice of hanging ratio reduces. What hanging ratio does change, once the target is held fixed, is the bill: twine per hung metre falls from 6.67 m at a ratio of 0.30 to 2.86 m at 0.70, and a 50-mesh-deep net hangs 6.83 m deep at 0.30 against 2.93 m at 0.70. The selection rule is therefore to set the band with mesh size and the target, and to spend hanging ratio on depth and twine cost, where it genuinely has leverage. The study is simulated, not measured.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24115132

A Minimum-Freeboard Bound for Small Open Boats: Wave Height, Bow Immersion and a Hand-Span Rule

The result. An open boat ships water when a wave encounter, at its worst moment, lifts the sea surface at the bow above the gunwale. This paper turns that moment into a simple bound that can be applied before leaving shore. The relative immersion at the bow is estimated as the sum of two parts: the crest height of a sinusoidal wave above still water, with no credit taken for the boat heaving with the wave, and the dip of the bow as the boat pitches with a stated fraction of the wave slope over a stated wavelength of 30 m. The sum is adjusted by a stated factor for bow shape. Boarding is taken to begin when this immersion reaches the loaded freeboard. For a 5.0 m boat of standard bow shape, a loaded freeboard of 0.30 m is exceeded at a wave height of 0.439 m; a freeboard of 0.20 m is exceeded at 0.293 m, and one of 0.40 m at 0.585 m. In units a fisherman can measure on his or her own hand, taking a stretched hand span as 0.20 m, the rule for the 5.0 m boat is that the largest wave that can be met without boarding is about 1.46 times the loaded freeboard, so one and a half spans of freeboard meet about 2.2 spans of wave. Bow shape matters at the same order as loading: at a length of 6.0 m and a freeboard of 0.30 m, a fine bow raises the limiting wave height to 0.490 m while a full, blunt bow lowers it to 0.362 m. Longer boats need more freeboard for the same wave, because the bow sits further from the pitching centre. The study is simulated, not measured, and the bound is deliberately conservative in the ways stated in the text.

A Closing Bound for Beach Seining: The Largest Seine a Standard Hauling Team Can Close, and the Haul Rate Longer Seines Require

The result. A beach seine traps fish in a pocket between the net and the shore, and the fish leave the way they came in: around the ends, through the gap that stays open until the haul is nearly finished. This paper originates a closing bound for that process. The seine is modelled as a semicircle against a straight beach, both ends are hauled toward each other at a speed limited by the hauling team's strength working against water drag on the net, and every fish is assumed to head straight for the nearer end at an effective speed of 0.25 m/s for the whole closing time, which makes the computed escape fraction an upper bound. Against a stated design bound of one fish in five escaping (a fraction of 0.20), the largest seine a standard team of two haulers per end, pulling 400 N per end, can close in time is 25 m: at 25 m the model's escape fraction is 0.1677, and at 30 m it has already risen to 0.2471. For longer seines the paper inverts the calculation and gives the minimum haul rate. The required haul speed turns out to be almost independent of length — 0.768 m/s at 40 m of seine and only 0.821 m/s at 200 m — but the force needed to haul that fast against a longer net grows in proportion to length, from 615 N per end (4 haulers) at 40 m to 1,615 N per end (9 haulers) at 100 m and 3,283 N per end (17 haulers) at 200 m. The bound is sensitive to fish speed: the limiting length is 40 m if the fish's sustained speed is 0.3 m/s and only 10 m if it is 0.8 m/s. The practical reading is that closing speed is bought with hauling force, not technique, and that long seines cannot be saved by hauling harder at any realistic team size — they stand or fall on keeping fish from making for the ends at all. The study is simulated, not measured.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24115152

Sizing the Outrigger Float of a Single-Outrigger Canoe: Ama Volume, Crossbeam Length and Heel Limits

The result. A single-outrigger canoe depends for most of its stability on one small float, the ama, held away from the hull by a crossbeam. Builders size that float by inheritance and by eye, and the penalty for undersizing is paid in a capsize or in a boat that cannot be worked. This paper derives a sizing rule from statics and applies it to three crew and load cases. The rule has two parts. First, a reserve-buoyancy requirement: the righting moment available when the ama is pressed to full immersion, equal to water density times gravity times ama volume times the distance from hull centreline to ama centreline, must reach twice the stated heeling moment, the factor of two being a stated dynamic allowance. Second, a heel check: the equilibrium heel under the stated heeling moment, from the combined stiffness of the hull and the immersed ama, must stay below a stated limit of 10 degrees. For a two-crew canoe of 260 kg total mass facing a heeling moment of 715 N m, the rule requires an ama of 48.6 litres at a centreline distance of 3.0 m, of diameter 0.153 m over a length of 3.3 m; the corresponding volumes are 58.3 litres at 2.5 m and 41.6 litres at 3.5 m. The one-crew case requires 24.3 litres and the three-crew case 80.4 litres at the same 3.0 m distance. In every case the heel check is met with a wide margin — equilibrium heels range from 0.54 degrees to 1.47 degrees across all cases and beam distances — and a float sized by the heel check alone would be under 2 litres, so it is the reserve-buoyancy requirement, not heel stiffness, that sets the size of a safe ama. The capacity when heeled away from the ama, so that the float is lifted rather than pressed, is smaller than the pressed capacity in every case and is tabulated as a warning. The study is simulated, not measured.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24115174

The Soak-Time Peak: Why a Longer Soak Does Not Always Catch More in a Baited Fish Trap

The result. A widely repeated rule of thumb holds that a baited fish trap left in the water longer always holds more fish, so the best soak is the longest soak that weather and schedule allow. This study tests that claim with a simple dynamical model in which fish enter at a rate set by the remaining strength of the bait and by the space left in the trap, and leave through escapement and predation at a rate proportional to the number already inside. The claim does not survive the arithmetic. Under the baseline assumptions the catch rises to a peak of 19.43 fish at 18.04 hours and then falls: a trap hauled at 48 hours holds 12.86 fish, which is 31.9 percent fewer than it held at 24 hours and fewer than the 18.49 fish it already held at 12 hours. Halving the peak soak time costs 12.8 percent of the peak catch; doubling it costs 17.2 percent. The peak arrives earlier when bait decays faster, when escapement is higher, or when predators learn the trap, and later in cold water with slow bait decay (28.98 hours) or with a tight, low-loss funnel (25.08 hours). The paper gives a practical soak-time rule: haul near the computed peak for the conditions at hand, and treat the interval over which the model holds at least 95 percent of the peak catch — 11.94 to 26.34 hours in the baseline case — as the working window. This study is simulated, not measured.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24115178

A Trip Fuel-Planning Rule for a Small Outboard Fishing Boat: Speed, Load, and Why a Larger Engine Does Not Burn Proportionally More

The result. This simulated study builds a trip fuel-planning rule for a 6.0 m semi-displacement open fishing boat powered by a small petrol outboard, and uses it to test the common belief that fitting a larger engine raises trip fuel consumption in proportion to the increase in rated power. Required shaft power is modelled as a function of speed and total displacement, and fuel consumption is modelled through a specific consumption curve that worsens at low load fraction, so that an engine working well below its rated output is penalised for part-throttle inefficiency but not charged for power it is not producing. For the headline case — a total trip distance of 20 nautical miles at 7 knots with a medium load — the 15 hp engine burns 4.434 litres for the trip, and the planning rule proposed here, fuel to carry equals computed trip burn multiplied by a reserve factor of 1.50, gives 6.651 litres to carry. Speed is expensive in this model: the same 20 nautical mile trip burns 3.110 litres at 5 knots and 5.421 litres at 9 knots. Load matters almost as much: at 7 knots over 20 nautical miles the trip burn rises from 3.772 litres with a light load to 5.135 litres with a heavy load. The larger-engine belief does not survive in its proportional form. The 25 hp engine, whose rated power is two thirds higher again than the 15 hp engine, burns only 10.9 percent more fuel on the headline trip (4.919 litres against 4.434 litres), with the extra consumption ranging from 6.2 percent at 5 knots to 14.2 percent at 8 knots across the cases computed. A larger engine does burn more for the same trip in every case computed here, because it runs at a lower load fraction and carries extra weight, but the increase is a fraction of what a proportional rule would predict, and buying fuel, tankage, or range on a proportional assumption would substantially overstate the cost of the larger engine. The study is simulated, not measured.

A Sizing Rule for Fish Trap Funnel Throats: Throat Diameter and Cone Angle Against Entry and Escape

The result. A trap funnel has to do two opposed things: admit the fish that are wanted and then refuse to let them back out. This study originates a sizing rule for the funnel entrance cone by modelling both duties from stated assumptions. Entry is modelled as a rising function of the ratio between throat diameter and the body depth of the target fish; escape over a 24-hour soak is modelled from the throat area a searching fish is presented with, the cone half-angle that guides it back to the throat, and the cone length it must traverse. A design is accepted when modelled entry probability is at least 0.80 and modelled escapement over 24 hours is at most 0.10. A throat equal to the body depth admits only half the target fish (entry probability 0.50), so the throat must exceed the depth: the recommended window runs from 1.18 to 1.40 times the body depth for every target size computed. The cone angle ceiling tightens sharply as the target fish grows: at the stated standard, a 60 mm fish tolerates angles up to 40 degrees, a 100 mm fish up to 27 degrees at its narrowest recommended throat, and a 150 mm fish only 12 degrees. Computed windows are tabulated for target body depths of 60, 80, 100, 120 and 150 mm. This study is simulated, not measured.

Choose the Line by Its Knotted Strength, Not the Number on the Spool: A Weakest-Link Table for Handline and Longline Gear

The result. This simulated study performs a weakest-link accounting for handline and longline gear and derives the rule that line should be chosen by its knotted strength, not by the breaking strain printed on the spool. Each knot is assigned a stated retained-strength fraction chosen inside a published range: 0.93 for a Palomar knot, 0.82 for a Uni knot, 0.75 for an improved clinch knot, 0.65 for a blood knot join, and 0.50 for a plain overhand knot. A fighting fish is modelled as imposing a peak load of 3.0 times its body weight. On a line whose spool figure is 20 kgf, the knotted strength is 18.6 kgf with a Palomar knot, holding a fish of 6.2 kg under the load model, but only 15.0 kgf with an improved clinch knot (a fish of 5.0 kg) and only 10.0 kgf with an overhand knot (a fish of 3.33 kg). Using the spool figure in place of the knotted strength overstates the gear's capacity by 7.5 percent for the Palomar knot, 33.3 percent for the improved clinch knot, 53.8 percent for the blood knot, and 100.0 percent for the overhand knot; the spool figure promises a fish of 6.67 kg on the 20 kgf line, where the overhand-knotted gear holds 3.33 kg. The accounting also shows that the main line is often not the weakest link at all: a 20 kgf main line joined to a 12 kgf leader fails at the knotted leader, at 11.16 kgf even with Palomar knots, holding a fish of 3.72 kg. Strengthening the main line while leaving the leader and its knots unchanged adds nothing. The study is simulated, not measured.

A Degradable Escape Panel Specification for Fish Traps: A Cotton-Twine Fastener That Ends Ghost Fishing Within Thirty Days

The result. A lost fish trap does not stop fishing. Its bait draws a first catch, the catch itself becomes bait for the next, and the trap can go on taking fish long after its owner has written it off. This study first models that process: with a first cycle of 14 fish, six-day cycles, and each cycle retaining 0.78 of the previous cycle's catch as self-bait, a lost trap fishes for 97.2 days and takes 62.5 fish before its per-cycle catch fades below a quarter of a fish. The study then originates a specification for a degradable escape panel intended to stop a lost trap within a chosen deadline of 30 days: a buoyant 200 mm by 200 mm panel held shut by four ties of untreated cotton twine, sized so that seawater decay brings the twine's strength down to the panel's steady opening load at the right time, neither during normal service nor long after the deadline. The computed fastener rule accepts twine diameters from 2.35 to 2.75 mm; the nominal 2.5 mm tie, with an initial breaking strength of 12.5 kgf and a strength half-life of 8.75 days, opens the panel at 24.5 days, still shows a safety factor of 1.77 against the greatest service load after the longest permitted 10-day service interval, and cuts the lost trap's fishing life by 74.8 percent, preventing 21.9 of the 62.5 fish (35.1 percent) the unprotected trap would have taken. This study is simulated, not measured.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24115236

An Anchor Sizing and Scope Rule for Small Fishing Boats: Holding in Sand and Mud at 20 and 30 Knots of Wind

The result. This simulated study derives an anchor sizing and scope rule for a 6.0 m open fishing boat, by setting the load the weather imposes on the boat against the holding force an anchor of a given weight can develop in sand and in mud, with holding reduced at short scope. Wind load is computed from a stated windage area and the square of wind speed; a stated current of 1.0 knot adds a constant load of 14.94 kgf; and the steady total is doubled by a stated surge allowance to give a design load of 66.90 kgf in 20 knots of wind and 113.18 kgf in 30 knots. Holding is modelled as anchor weight multiplied by a bottom factor at a scope of 7:1 — 25 in clean sand and 10 in soft mud — multiplied further by a scope factor of 0.40 at 3:1, 0.75 at 5:1, 1.00 at 7:1, and 1.15 at 10:1. At a scope of 7:1 the anchor weight required is 2.68 kg in sand and 6.69 kg in mud at 20 knots, rising to 4.53 kg in sand and 11.32 kg in mud at 30 knots. Scope is worth more than weight in this model: at 30 knots in sand, shortening the scope from 7:1 to 3:1 raises the required weight from 4.53 kg to 11.32 kg, exactly the weight that soft mud demands at full scope, while in mud at 3:1 the requirement reaches 28.30 kg, beyond what such a boat can reasonably carry. The rule is therefore twofold: size the anchor for the worst bottom and the worst weather in the table that the boat will actually face, and treat scope as the first remedy when holding is in doubt, because veering more rode multiplies the holding of the anchor already aboard. The study is simulated, not measured.

The market stall — 17 papers

Produce that reaches the stall in condition, stalls laid out so people can reach and choose, and weights and prices that are fair.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24115158

Spoilage Against Selling Time: A Temperature-Dependent Decay Model for Leafy Greens and a Break-Even Rule for How Much to Bring to Market

The result. A seller of leafy greens must decide in the morning how much to bring. Too little loses sales while stock is fresh; too much loses saleable quality while it waits. This paper models that trade-off explicitly. Saleable fraction decays exponentially with time since harvest, at a rate rising with temperature according to a Q10 factor. The model yields three outputs. First, saleable fraction by temperature and age, with a half-life of 23.10 hours at 20 °C, falling to 9.24 hours at 30 °C and 5.85 hours at 35 °C. Second, a costing of unsold stock: at 30 °C, bringing 100 bunches against demand of 85 sells 80.23, loses 19.77 to spoilage, and carries an unsold cost of $23.73. Third, a break-even rule: for demand of 85, the computed optima are 90 bunches at 15 °C, 93 at 20 °C, 98 at 25 °C, 107 at 30 °C, and 124 at 35 °C — loading factors of 1.06, 1.09, 1.15, 1.26, and 1.46 times the forecast. A common shortcut, dividing demand by the saleable fraction at mid-market, overstates the quantity at every temperature, by 5 bunches at 15 °C and 49 at 35 °C, because early sales remove stock before it can spoil.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24115176

End-of-Day Discounting for a Decaying Perishable: A Triggered Single-Markdown Schedule That Maximises Computed Revenue

The result. When should a seller of perishable produce start discounting, and by how much? Too early or too deeply gives away margin on stock that would have sold at full price; too late or too shallowly lets stock spoil that a lower price would have moved. This paper computes the answer for a stated case: leafy greens on a 6-hour day at 28 °C, full price $3.00 per bunch, baseline demand of 11 bunches per hour, and a demand rate that rises with the discount. The originated result is a triggered, single-markdown schedule. At the midpoint, count the stock remaining. If it exceeds 39.6 bunches — 1.2 times the 33 bunches expected to sell at full price in the remaining three hours — apply one markdown of 20 percent for the rest of the day. Otherwise hold full price. In the base case, starting with 100 bunches, full price all day gives revenue of $198.00, sells 66.00 bunches, leaves 13.68 unsold, and loses 20.32 to spoilage. The triggered schedule gives $213.05, sells 80.52, leaves 0.44, and adds $15.05, an uplift of 7.6 percent. Alternatives do less well: a two-step schedule (10 percent off at the midpoint, then 30 percent for the final 1.5 hours) reaches $210.87, and a markdown confined to the final 1.5 hours peaks at $206.02. At starting stocks of 60 and 80 bunches, no late markdown improves on full price at all — which is why the schedule is triggered by a count rather than applied by habit.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24115188

Harvest Timing Against Market Timing: Quality-Loss Bounds for Morning-of and Evening-Before Harvest of Leafy Greens, and a Rule for When Each Wins

The result. Leafy greens for a morning market are cut either the evening before or early on the market day itself. The evening cut spreads labour; the morning cut is fresher but demands pre-dawn work. This paper puts computed bounds on the quality difference and derives a rule for choosing. Quality retained is exp(−A), where A is the integral of a temperature-dependent decay rate from harvest to the time of interest. Three routes are computed across daytime temperatures of 20, 25, 30, and 35 °C: evening-before at ambient overnight (15 hours to opening), evening-before in an 8 °C cool box (15 hours), and morning-of harvest (2.5 hours). At opening, morning-of retains 0.9576, 0.9339, 0.8974, and 0.8428 across those days — a loss of 4.2 to 15.7 percent. Evening-before at ambient retains only 0.7707, 0.6624, 0.5214, and 0.3571 — a loss of 22.9 to 64.3 percent. The cool box retains 0.8608 on every day, because its temperature does not depend on the weather. Two thresholds are originated. On quality alone, cooled evening ties morning-of when the temperature at the morning harvest reaches 27.6 °C. On total penalty per bunch — quality loss valued at the $3.00 price, plus $0.18 per bunch early-labour premium for the morning route and $0.07 for cool-box handling — the two routes tie on a 30 °C day, each at $0.488, with the morning route cheaper below that temperature and the cool box cheaper above it. Ambient evening harvest, despite no extra cash cost, carries the largest penalty at every temperature, reaching $1.929 per bunch on a 35 °C day. The rule is: do not rely on an ambient overnight hold in warm weather; choose between a morning cut and a cooled evening cut by which side of roughly 30 °C the day sits.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24115214

Stall Shading and Wilting: How Much Selling Time Shade Buys for Leafy Produce, and When It Pays

The result. Leafy produce displayed at an open market loses water continuously, and once cumulative loss passes a visible wilting point it becomes difficult or impossible to sell at full price. This paper models that process for a single stated case — a 200 g lettuce head, with unsaleability set at a loss of 5.0% of fresh mass — under open sun and under shade cloth of three transmissions. Water loss is driven by the vapour pressure difference between the leaf and the air, with leaf temperature rising with the fraction of sun that reaches it. On a typical day defined here as 30°C air temperature and 45% relative humidity, the modelled time to wilting is 2.756 hours in open sun, 3.287 hours under cloth transmitting 0.7 of the sun, 3.727 hours under cloth transmitting 0.5, and 4.259 hours under cloth transmitting 0.3. Medium shade, at transmission 0.5, therefore buys 0.971 hours, a gain of 35.2%. On a hot day at 35°C and 35% relative humidity the times are shorter for every treatment and the absolute gain from medium shade falls to 0.568 hours, even though wilting arrives sooner and matters more. On a mild day at 25°C and 60% relative humidity the open-sun time is already 4.287 hours and medium shade extends it to 6.275 hours. An economic calculation for a 6.0-hour market, 20 kg of stock priced at $6.00 per kilogram, and shade costing $0.55 per day shows shade paying by a wide margin whenever the market lasts longer than the open-sun wilting time. The break-even stock value for medium shade is only $3.40 on the typical day and $5.81 on the hot day. The originated rule is therefore conditional, not universal: first compare market length with open-sun wilting time; if the market ends before that time, shade buys no saleable produce in this model, and if it runs beyond it, even a small leafy stock justifies medium shade, with heavier shade reserved for long markets on hot days where the extra hour is actually used.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24115238

Display Height and Reach: The Reachable Fraction of a Market Stall and a Stepped Display Rule

The result. A flat market table hides much of its own stock. This paper calculates, from stated reach assumptions, what fraction of a flat display lies within a customer's comfortable reach, within a customer's maximum reach, and within a vendor's reach from the opposite side. The customer is assigned a comfortable reach of 40 cm beyond the table edge and a maximum reach of 60 cm. The vendor is assigned 50 cm comfortable and 70 cm maximum. On a flat table 120 cm deep, only 0.3333 of the stock, uniformly distributed by depth, lies within customer comfortable reach, and only 0.5000 lies within customer maximum reach. The middle 30 cm is beyond comfortable reach from either side, although it can be reached at full stretch by someone. On a table 150 cm deep a dead zone appears: 20 cm in the middle is beyond maximum reach from either side. Raising the display surface makes matters worse, with comfortable reach falling by 4 cm for each 10 cm the surface rises above 95 cm. A sloped board does not by itself solve the problem, because reach along the slope is still the limiting distance. The paper therefore originates a stepped display rule: three tiers, each 32 cm deep, each step 18 cm high, with a clearance penalty of 6 cm per lower tier in the reach calculation. That design has a total depth of 96 cm and places 0.9583 of its stock within customer comfortable reach and 1.0000 within customer maximum reach, an improvement of 62.5 percentage points in the comfortable fraction over the 120 cm flat table, while the vendor reaches the whole design comfortably from the back. The rule for general use is to size each tier at or below 32 cm, keep the number of tiers at three under the stated clearances, and treat any flat depth beyond 90 cm as a display that requires either vendor service for most of its stock or a stepped rebuild.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24115254

Stacking Depth and Crushing: Maximum Safe Depth for Ripe Tomatoes and the Cost of Exceeding It

The result. Stacking produce deeper makes a stall look abundant and saves table space, but the fruit at the bottom carries the fruit above it. This paper models that load for a stated produce type, the ripe tomato, and derives both the depth at which damage begins and the depth at which a stated bruise threshold is crossed. Each tomato is assigned a mass of 0.12 kg and a diameter of 7.0 cm, giving a layer height of 5.95 cm. Load on a bottom fruit is the weight of the column above it multiplied by a load concentration factor of 1.4, and the bruise threshold for a ripe tomato is set at a force of 12.0 N per fruit, with a contact diameter of 1.5 cm. Under these assumptions each additional layer adds 1.65 N to the bottom fruit. Damage in the model begins well before the threshold is formally crossed: the bottom layer damage fraction is 0.0000 at six layers, 0.1378 at seven layers, and 0.2904 at eight layers, where the force is 11.54 N and the pressure is 65.3 kPa. At nine layers, a depth of 53.6 cm, the force reaches 13.18 N and exceeds the threshold. The recommended operating limit originated here is therefore six layers, a depth of 35.7 cm, not eight. Stacking a 10 kg pile of ripe tomatoes at $5.00 per kilogram to ten layers instead of six raises the overall damage fraction to 0.1467 and the value loss to $7.33 per pile, and stacking to twelve layers raises the loss to $12.98. Firmer produce tolerates more: a firm tomato with a threshold of 22.0 N shows no bottom-layer damage in the model until eleven layers, and an apple with a threshold of 35.0 N and a mass of 0.18 kg shows a bottom-layer damage fraction of only 0.0070 at eleven layers. The depth limit must therefore be set by ripeness, not by crate size.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24115263

Transport Damage by Vehicle and Road: A Viability Distance for Fragile Produce

The result. A seller carrying fragile produce to a small market faces a choice that is usually made by habit: carry it, cycle it, put it on a cart, or pay for a motor vehicle. This paper replaces habit with arithmetic. For a 20 kg load of firm-ripe tomatoes carried bare in a rigid basket, damage is modelled as $D(d) = 1 - \exp(-kd)$, where the rate $k$ is built from a stated count of jolts per kilometre and a stated probability of bruising per jolt, multiplied by a road factor. Five modes are compared on three surfaces. The new result is a viability distance for each combination: the distance at which damage reaches 15%, taken here as the point at which the load stops being worth transporting in that way. On paved road the viability distances are 20.31 km on foot, 13.54 km by pickup, 10.83 km by bicycle, 6.50 km by motorcycle, and 4.64 km by cart. On rough earth they fall to 6.35 km, 4.23 km, 3.39 km, 2.03 km, and 1.45 km respectively. The ranking does not change with the road; only the scale does. Speed, on damage alone, does not protect produce. The gentlest mode is the slowest one, and the unsprung cart is the most damaging mode on every surface examined.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24115280

How Far a Load Can Survive: A Combined Bound from Jolting, Time, and Temperature Rise

The result. Mechanical damage alone gives a misleading answer to the question of how far fragile produce can be carried, because the gentlest mode is usually the slowest, and slowness exposes the load to heat for longer. This paper combines three effects into a single survival bound for firm-ripe tomatoes picked at 24 °C: jolting, which accumulates per kilometre; elapsed time; and a rising load temperature, which accelerates quality loss. The bound requires the surviving saleable fraction $S(d) = \exp(-k d - \int_0^{d/v} r(T(t))\,dt)$ to remain at or above 0.80. Solved numerically, the maximum viable distances on paved road are 10.67 km walking, 11.90 km cycling, and 4.40 km by cart. On gravel they are 8.33 km walking, 7.29 km cycling, and 2.86 km by cart. On rough earth they are 5.96 km walking, 4.33 km cycling, and 1.77 km by cart. The combined bound also produces a reversal: cycling leads on paved road, where its speed shortens heat exposure, while walking leads on gravel and rough earth, where the bicycle's higher jolting rate outweighs its speed. For the walking carrier on paved road, heat and time account for 0.618 of the total loss exponent at the maximum distance, so a jolt-only calculation overstates the walking range by more than half.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24115299

Packaging That Pays for Itself: A Break-Even Rule for Cushioning a Market Load

The result. Sellers are routinely advised to pack fragile produce better, as if cushioning were always worth its cost. It is not. This paper derives the break-even arithmetic for five packaging options applied to a 20 kg load of ripe tomatoes worth $30.00, using damage rates for five transport situations. Packaging is modelled as reducing the damage rate $k$ by an effectiveness fraction $e$, at a stated cost per load. The saving at distance $d$ is the value of the load multiplied by the damage prevented. The paper originates a break-even rule in two forms: an exact distance found from the full damage function, and a short-distance approximation, $d_{be} \approx \text{Cost} / (V \cdot e \cdot k)$, that a seller can evaluate mentally. On a bicycle journey over gravel, newspaper wrap breaks even at 2.07 km, banana-leaf cushioning at 2.22 km, straw or shredded paper bedding at 2.61 km, foam net sleeves at 6.51 km, and moulded pulp trays at 6.97 km. By cart over gravel, every option breaks even within 2.99 km. By pickup on paved road, foam nets do not break even until 14.65 km and pulp trays not until 15.69 km. Expensive packaging on a short, gentle trip is a loss; cheap packaging on a rough, damaging trip pays for itself within the first few kilometres. The deciding quantity is not the price of the packaging alone, but the product $V \cdot e \cdot k$.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24115315

Packaging That Does Not Pay: Sealed Film, Respiration Heat, and the Shortened Saleable Life of Market Produce

The result. A common belief at small produce markets is that sealing produce in plastic film keeps it fresher. This paper tests that belief with a simple thermal and humidity model for 1 kg packs of tomatoes, lettuce, and carrots held at a market stall. Sealing reduces the rate at which respiration heat can leave the pack, so the produce warms itself, respires faster, and consumes its own saleable life more quickly. In shade at an ambient temperature of 22 °C, the computed temperature rise from sealing is 0.655 K for tomatoes, 1.761 K for lettuce, and 0.407 K for carrots, with corresponding reductions in saleable life of 5.3%, 13.6%, and 3.3%. In intermittent sun, represented by an effective ambient temperature of 28 °C, a sealed pack of lettuce reaches 32.556 °C and its saleable life falls to 1.74 days, a 56.6% reduction relative to an open pack in shade. The air inside a sealed bag reaches saturation in about 2.0 minutes for tomatoes, so the humidity benefit claimed for film is exhausted almost immediately, after which the bag holds saturated air against the surface for the rest of the trading period. The belief fails as a general rule. It holds in one narrow case identified here: for leafy produce sold before wilting becomes binding, sealing prevents water loss that would otherwise end saleability in 1.79 days. Outside that case, and in particular for fruiting and root produce held for more than a day, open or ventilated packaging preserves more saleable life at no packaging cost.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24115334

Weighing Accuracy and Short Weight: What a Small Systematic Error Transfers Over a Market Season

The result. A scale error that is barely visible in a single transaction becomes a large sum when it repeats, in the same direction, for a whole season. This paper models a market stall selling 250 kg of produce per day over 96 trading days, at an average price of $4.00 per kg, for a season total of 24,000 kg and honest revenue of $96,000. A systematic short weight of 1% transfers $960 from buyers to the vendor over the season, 2% transfers $1,920, and 5% transfers $4,800. Per transaction the same errors are only 17.9 g, 35.7 g, and 89.3 g on an average purchase of 1.786 kg, worth 7.1, 14.3, and 35.7 cents respectively, which explains why no individual buyer is likely to notice or contest them. The cost is symmetric in retail value for over-weight: a vendor whose scale gives 1%, 2%, or 5% too much gives away produce with a retail value of $960, $1,920, or $4,800 over the season, at a direct stock cost of $576, $1,152, or $2,880. Accuracy is therefore not a matter of fairness in the abstract only. It is a priced quantity, and the price of a small fixed bias, in either direction, is set by season throughput. The paper also computes how many test purchases are needed to distinguish a bias from random weighing variation, showing that a 2% bias can be separated from a 1.5% random standard deviation with about three test purchases, while a 1% bias needs about nine.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24115344

Checking a Spring Scale With What Is at Hand: A Water-Litre Calibration Method and Its Certified Bound

The result. A market vendor who owns no test weights still owns, in effect, a reference mass: a measured litre of water. This paper originates a complete checking procedure based on that reference, and, more importantly, computes an honest bound for what the procedure can certify. Water is not exactly 1,000 g per litre except near 4 °C; at 20 °C a litre has a mass of 998.2 g, at 25 °C 997.04 g, and at 30 °C 995.65 g, so treating a litre as exactly a kilogram introduces an error of 0.18% to 0.435% across that range. That error is small compared with the other errors of a cheap spring scale. Reading resolution contributes 2.5% at a 1 kg load on a typical 5 kg scale with 50 g graduations, tilting the scale or its load by 10 degrees makes it read 1.519% low, and a 20 K temperature shift changes the indication by 0.5%. Adding the controlled error components for the prescribed procedure gives a worst-case bound of 6.23% at a 1 kg load, and a root-sum-square figure of 3.39%. Checking at a 3 kg load, by filling the litre measure three times, tightens the worst-case bound to 3.9%. The water check therefore certifies a spring scale against gross error — the class of error that does the largest season damage — but it does not certify a scale to 1% or 2%. The paper states the procedure, the correction for water temperature, the controls for angle and temperature, and the exact form of words a vendor can honestly use about a scale that passes.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24115357

Pricing by the Piece against Pricing by Weight: Buyer Selection, Fairness, and a Two-Size Grading Rule for Apples

The result. A vendor who sells apples by the piece charges the same for fruit of different weights. A vendor who sells by weight charges in proportion to what each buyer takes. This paper compares the two methods for apples with a stated weight distribution, a stated price per kilogram, and a stated pattern of buyer choice. When fruit is allocated at random, the methods return almost the same revenue. When buyers choose the largest fruit, piece pricing transfers a substantial sum from vendor to buyer: the vendor receives $109.50 for fruit that would return $124.49 by weight in a day, a loss of $14.99, or 9.99 cents per apple sold. The same piece price charges different buyers between $3.07 and $5.74 per kilogram. Splitting stock into two sizes at the median weight and pricing each size separately reduces the loss to $9.34 per day, but grading labour costs $6.00 per day, so the net benefit is −$0.35 per day. From this the paper derives a grading rule: grade into two sizes only when the coefficient of variation of fruit weight exceeds a break-even value set by labour cost, the residual loss after grading, and the value at stake. For the stated terms that value is 0.2428, about a standard deviation of 43.7 grams at a mean of 180 grams. The stated apples, with a coefficient of variation of 0.2286, fall just below the line, so grading them on these terms does not pay.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24115367

Queues at a Single-Vendor Stall: Waiting through the Morning Peak, the Point at Which Customers Leave Faster than They Are Served, and a Rule for a Second Pair of Hands

The result. A single-vendor stall serves one customer at a time, while arrivals rise through the morning, peak, and fall away. This paper computes queue length and waiting time for a stated arrival profile and a service rate of 30 customers per hour, using steady-state queue formulas for each hour and a minute-by-minute fluid calculation across the morning. At 20 arrivals per hour the mean wait before service is 4.0 minutes. At 28 per hour it is 28.0 minutes, with a mean of 13.07 customers waiting. In the peak hour, at 35 arrivals per hour against service of 30 per hour, no steady state exists and the queue grows while that rate lasts. Adding abandonment, with mean patience of 10 minutes, waiting customers leave at 6 per hour for each customer waiting, so they leave faster than the vendor serves once the queue passes 5. In the fluid morning, abandonment also caps the peak queue at 0.83 and limits morning abandonment to 4.39 customers. A second pair of hands for three peak hours costs $45.00, retains those 4.39 customers worth $10.53, and returns −$34.47 on the stated morning. A scan over sustained rates shows help paying only at high rates: over three hours at a sustained 38 per hour it retains 22.67 customers worth $54.40, for a net $9.40. The originated rule is to hire peak help only when the sustained arrival rate is high enough that retained customers at their contribution exceed the wage — on these terms, between a sustained 35 and 38 per hour, not on the strength of one busy hour in a moderate morning.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24115378

Rounding and Change: What Coin Denominations Cost a Produce Vendor over a Day and a Season, and the Arithmetic of Giving Produce Instead of Small Change

The result. Cash prices at a market stall rarely fall exactly on the coins handed over, so somebody absorbs the difference. This paper computes what common rounding practices cost or gain a stated vendor over a day and over a season of 96 trading days, with 280 transactions per day of which 112 are in cash. Rounding each cash total to the nearest 5 cents is fair on average: mean gain in simulation is $0.006 per day and $0.60 per season, with a daily standard deviation of $0.148 and an approximate 95 percent season range from −$27.29 to $28.50. Rounding systematically one way is different. Always rounding down costs an average of 2 cents per cash transaction, $2.24 per day, and $215.04 per season. Always rounding up gains the same, at customers' expense. Price points carry a larger lever: moving every item price up to the next multiple of 25 cents adds a mean of 11.99 cents per item, or $0.384 per transaction at 3.2 items per transaction. The paper then prices giving produce instead of small change, and originates a rule: give produce only when its marginal cost is below the change owed while its retail value is above the change owed. A garnish costing 3 cents with retail value 12 cents, given in place of an average 4 cents owed, saves 1 cent per use; across a season it saves $19.35 against a cash cost of $77.41, while handing customers $232.24 of retail value. A whole extra apple, costing 41 cents in place of 5 cents owed, fails the rule by a wide margin.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24115392

A Cold Box Sized for One Day of Trading: An Ice Sizing Rule per Kilogram of Produce per Trading Hour

The result. A trader selling soft produce from an insulated box must decide each morning how much ice to buy. Too little leaves produce warm; too much ties up cash, space and carrying weight. This paper originates a sizing rule in units a trader can use: kilograms of ice per kilogram of produce per trading hour, as a function of ambient temperature, trading duration and insulation. The rule is derived from a heat balance on a stated reference box — expanded polystyrene with 25 mm walls, mean surface area 1.50 m² and target interior 4 °C — carrying 20 kg of produce. For an 8-hour day it gives 10.417 kg of ice at 20 °C ambient, 15.47 kg at 30 °C and 20.524 kg at 40 °C. At 30 °C that is 0.7735 kg per kilogram of produce, or 0.09669 kg per kilogram per hour. The ice at 30 °C costs $6.19 and, under the stated spoilage model, prevents $13.91 of value loss, for a net benefit of $7.72 on a $60.00 lot, or $2.25 saved per dollar spent on ice. At 20 °C the net benefit is only $0.98. The largest single heat load is not conduction through the walls but cooling the produce itself from ambient temperature to 4 °C. All results are computed from stated assumptions. SIMULATED, NOT MEASURED.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24115402

More Ice Is Not Always Better: Chilling Injury and Falling Saleable Value in Over-Iced Tropical Produce

The result. A widely held belief at small markets is that adding more ice to a cold box always increases the value that can be sold that day: colder, on this view, is safer. This paper shows by computation that the belief does not survive for tropical produce. Tropical crops are injured by cold itself, below crop-specific thresholds, and injury grows with depth below the threshold and time spent there. Using a stated model for a 20 kg load traded for 8 hours at 32 °C ambient, with interior temperature falling as the ice ratio r — ice mass divided by produce mass — rises, the paper computes heat loss, chilling injury, ice cost and net saleable value for banana, mango, papaya, avocado and, as a temperate control, lettuce. Banana reaches its best net value of $20.51 at r = 0.21, enters chilling conditions at r = 0.322, and at r = 1.0 retains only $1.73, a fall of 91.6% from its grid optimum. Mango peaks at $54.49 at r = 0.35 and falls to $25.00 at r = 1.0. Lettuce, with no chilling threshold above 0 °C in the model, loses only 8.6% between its grid optimum and r = 1.0, mainly through ice cost. The ice fraction above which added ice reduces value is therefore stated for each crop. All results are computed from stated assumptions. SIMULATED, NOT MEASURED.

Household manufactures — 17 papers

Soap, salt, syrup, candles, starch, lime and other goods made at home, with the quantities and tests stated plainly.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24115078

A strength test for wood-ash lye without instruments

The result. Wood-ash lye needs to fall within a narrow concentration band for soap-making, yet the household maker usually has no hydrometer or titration kit. The traditional substitute is the floating-egg test: lye is judged ready when a fresh egg floats with a small cap exposed. This paper calibrates that test in a physical model, derives a sizing rule for ash, water and leaching passes, and sets a failure bound. The central egg, at 1.060 g/mL, reaches neutral buoyancy at 65.2 g/L of alkali as potassium carbonate, but eggs spanning 1.030 to 1.090 g/mL indicate anywhere from 32.6 to 97.8 g/L at the same visual point, a range 65.2 g/L wide against a target band only 40.0 g/L wide. An egg floating with 5% exposed corresponds to 125.9 g/L, near the top of the target band of 90.0 to 130.0 g/L. The sizing rule for mixed household ash is 15 kg of ash per 10 L of water leached in 2 passes, giving 115.5 g/L. The same recipe across ash sources gives 64.2 to 154.0 g/L, a 2.4-fold spread. The claim that stronger lye is always better does not survive: dosing a fixed volume sized for mid-band lye with lye at 180 g/L leaves 89.7 g of excess potassium hydroxide equivalent per kilogram of tallow, an upper-bound free alkali content of 6.85% in the finished bar, 68.5 times a 0.1% reference limit.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24115084

One recipe does not fit every fat

The result. Household soap recipes are often transmitted as a single quantity of alkali per unit of fat, for use with whatever fat is at hand. This paper tests that practice against published saponification values for six common fats and shows, in numbers, that it does not survive. A fixed recipe of 140.0 g of sodium hydroxide per kilogram requires, in fact, 99.1 g for lard and 182.5 g for coconut oil, a range whose maximum is 1.84 times its minimum. On tallow the fixed recipe leaves 39.5 g of surplus alkali per kilogram, a free alkali content of 3.06% in the cured bar, 30.6 times a 0.1% reference limit. On coconut oil it falls short by 42.5 g and leaves 23.3% of the fat unsaponified. No single fixed quantity satisfies both extremes: the ceiling from the lowest-demand fat is 100.4 g per kilogram, while the floor from the highest-demand fat, allowing at most 8% unsaponified fat, is 167.9 g per kilogram, a gap of 67.5 g. The replacement rule is fat-specific: dose 95% of the requirement calculated from the fat's published value, giving 95.5 g per kilogram for tallow, 173.4 g for coconut oil and 118.8 g for a stated 70/30 tallow and coconut blend.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24115107

The curing bound: when is a bar of soap actually ready?

The result. Fresh soap is routinely judged ready by feel: it no longer feels slippery, and a touch to the tongue no longer produces a sharp sensation. This paper models the three processes curing must complete, water loss, decline of free alkali, and development of hardness, and tests those signs against the resulting timetable. For a stated 120.0 g bar measuring 80 by 55 by 25 mm, starting at 28.0% water and curing at 20 °C and 50% relative humidity, water falls to 20.2% at day 7, 15.6% at day 14 and 11.3% at day 28. Free alkali in a zero-superfat batch falls from 2.40% at demoulding to 0.43% at day 7 and reaches 0.1% only at day 22.9. The minimum cure satisfying all three criteria is 23 days for a zero-superfat batch and 21 days with a 5% lye discount. The traditional signs clear far earlier: the tongue-test proxy clears at day 9.2 for the zero-superfat batch and day 2.1 for the superfatted batch, and the slippery-feel proxy at day 5.1, a gap of 13.7 days between the tongue test clearing and the zero-superfat minimum. A hardness index from saturated fatty-acid content, H = 12 + 0.92 times the saturated percentage, gives 87.4 for coconut oil, 58.0 for tallow and 24.9 for olive oil, and shows that no cure duration compensates for a low-saturation blend.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24115130

Sizing a solar salt pan

The result. A household that can reach the sea often asks a deceptively simple question: how large must a solar salt pan be? This paper derives a sizing rule from a water balance rather than from custom or anecdote. Salt can leave a pan no faster than water does, so the salt production rate is the net evaporation rate multiplied by the ratio of salt to water in the feed brine. Net evaporation is gross evaporation reduced by a factor of 0.85 for concentrated brine, minus rainfall. For seawater at a mass fraction of 0.035, the rule gives 5.059 m2 of pan per kilogram of salt per day in an arid coastal climate with net evaporation of 5.45 mm per day, and 19.694 m2 per kilogram per day in a humid coastal climate with net evaporation of 1.4 mm per day. Spread over a dry season, a stated household need of 30.0 kg per year requires only 0.63 m2 in the arid case and 4.92 m2 in the humid case, provided the pan is refilled as it dries. The binding constraint is therefore not area in most coastal settings. It is the seasonal total of net evaporation. A pan on 20.0 m2 fails that household need when the season total of net evaporation falls below 41.4 mm, and it produces nothing at all when average rainfall reaches 5.95 mm per day against arid gross evaporation, or 3.4 mm per day against humid gross evaporation. A single 10.0 mm rain event on nearly saturated brine dilutes it from a mass fraction of 0.26 to 0.1418 and costs 1.83 extra days of evaporation in the arid case and 7.14 days in the humid case.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24115146

The break-even salinity for boiling brine

The result. Boiling seawater directly into salt is technically straightforward and energetically ruinous. This paper computes the fuel cost of boiling brine as a function of its starting concentration, using a latent heat of vaporisation of 2257 kJ per kg of water, a brine specific heat of 3.9 kJ per kg per K, dry wood at 15.0 MJ per kg, and a stove efficiency of 0.15. Boiling seawater at a salt mass fraction of 0.035 requires 70.586 MJ of heat and 31.371 kg of wood for each kilogram of salt. At a mass fraction of 0.2 the cost falls to 10.491 MJ and 4.662 kg of wood, and at 0.26, near saturation, to 7.549 MJ and 3.355 kg of wood. Against a stated household affordability bound of 5.0 kg of wood per kilogram of salt, the break-even concentration is a mass fraction of 0.1888, or 188.8 g per kg. Below that concentration, boiling from scratch does not pay under the stated bound; above it, fuel finishing becomes a reasonable use of gathered wood. The arithmetic points to a hybrid schedule: concentrate seawater by solar evaporation to a mass fraction of 0.2, which removes 0.8549 of the original water at no fuel cost, and then finish by boiling. That schedule reduces wood use by 85.1 percent compared with boiling seawater directly.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24115170

Evaporate to dryness is worse salt

The result. A common instruction in household salt making is to evaporate seawater completely and use everything that remains. This paper shows, by a fractional crystallisation model, that the instruction produces worse salt. The model starts from 1000 g of seawater containing 965 g of water and 35.0 g of dissolved salts, of which 27.0 g is common salt (NaCl). Each salt is held in solution until the remaining water falls below the amount its stated solubility requires. Calcium sulphate saturates early, when 500.0 g of water remains. NaCl begins to crystallise when 75.0 g of water remains, after a fraction of 0.9223 of the water has been removed, and it crystallises through a window that ends when magnesium chloride begins, at 7.04 g of water remaining. Stopping at 7.0 g of water remaining recovers 24.48 g of NaCl, a recovery of 90.7 percent, at a dry-crystal purity of 0.9532, or 0.9163 after allowing for retained mother liquor. Evaporating to complete dryness recovers the full 27.0 g of NaCl but delivers 35.0 g of mixed salts at a purity of 0.7714. Going to dryness therefore gains only 2.52 g of additional NaCl, which is 9.33 percent of the total NaCl present, while purity falls by 18.18 percentage points on a dry basis. The recommended stopping rule is to harvest when about 7.0 g of water remains for each 1000 g of seawater started — a fraction of 0.9927 of the original water removed — and to discard or separately use the remaining bitter liquor, known as bittern.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24115186

Can a small cane operation fuel itself?

The result. A small cane operation is often assumed to be fuel-self-sufficient because industrial mills burn their own bagasse. This paper tests that assumption at household and village scale, on a single-effect open pan, for a basis of 1000.0 kg of cane. A single-roller press recovering 54.0% of the soluble solids extracts 464.7 kg of juice and leaves 535.3 kg of bagasse at 60.1% moisture; it delivers 944.9 MJ of useful heat against 897.9 MJ needed to make 127.1 kg of syrup at 68% solids, a ratio of 1.052 — self-sufficient, but only just. A three-roller press without imbibition water, recovering 68.0% of the solids, also passes narrowly, at a ratio of 1.044. The same three-roller press with 180 kg of imbibition water recovers 82.0% of the solids and makes more syrup, 192.9 kg, yet fails: it delivers 1158.2 MJ against 1745.3 MJ needed, a ratio of 0.664, and requires 93.8 kg of outside wood per tonne of cane. Sun-drying the bagasse and improving the furnace narrows the deficit but does not close it. Only vapour reuse, modelled at a factor of 1.7, turns that high-extraction case self-sufficient, at a ratio of 1.33. The governing rule is therefore not extraction alone: on a single-effect pan, every kilogram of imbibition water must be evaporated again, and that second evaporation can consume more heat than the extra sugar is worth in fuel terms. All device and furnace figures in this paper are SIMULATED, NOT MEASURED.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24115216

Knowing the endpoint without a thermometer

The result. Syrup makers working without instruments judge the endpoint by hand: a thread drawn between the fingers, a drop set in cold water, a flake falling from the skimmer, or an egg floated in the pan. This paper calibrates each of those tests against final solids concentration, boiling temperature at three altitudes, and final moisture content, using stated correlations for density and boiling-point rise, and it prices the error of each test in fuel and in product loss. The target used throughout is syrup at 70% solids and 30% moisture. The thread test, the cold-water test, and the sheet test each centre between 70% and 72% solids with an error of ±3.0 percentage points of moisture. A litre measure weighed on a kitchen scale does better, at ±1.0 percentage points. The floating-egg test, judged by a coin-sized area of shell exposed, does not survive as a precision test: its nominal centre is 72.5% solids, but combining the stated ranges for egg density and for the exposed area an observer calls a coin gives a range from 54.0% to 95.0% solids. From juice at 15% solids, finishing 1 kg of syrup at the 70% target costs 1.980 kg of wood on the stated open-pan assumptions. Stopping at 65% solids saves 8.8% of that fuel but leaves 50 g of excess water in every kilogram. Running to 78% solids costs 14.1% extra fuel and, for a pure sucrose syrup cooled to 20 °C, puts an upper bound of 331.3 g per kilogram into crystals that fall out of solution. The recommended household method is a weighed litre as the primary test, with the thread test as a cross-check. All apparatus figures are SIMULATED, NOT MEASURED.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24115243

Why household crystallisation yields cap where they do

The result. Household and small-scale sugar makers commonly recover much less crystal from a pan of concentrated syrup than the sugar it contains, and the shortfall is often blamed on technique. This paper shows, by mass balance, that most of the shortfall is structural. From a stated massecuite of 100 kg at 93% solids and 85% purity — 79.05 kg of sucrose and 13.95 kg of non-sucrose solids — the theoretical first-strike crystal yield, with the mother liquor exhausted to 40% purity, is 69.75 kg of sucrose, which is 88.2% of the sucrose present and 69.8% of the massecuite mass. The remaining 9.30 kg of sucrose, 11.8% of the total, stays dissolved in 30.25 kg of mother liquor and cannot be removed by any amount of stirring, cooling, or draining, because the non-sucrose solids hold it in a liquor of that purity. Retention then taxes the result a second time: draining in cloth or a basket leaves 18.0% of the mother liquor on the crystals, 5.44 kg, dropping product purity to 95.0%, while a household basket centrifuge leaving 5.0% gives a product purity of 98.5%. After a stated handling loss and washing off the retained liquor, the practical single-strike cap for a drained, washed product is 66.68 kg, which is 84.4% of the sucrose and 66.7% of the massecuite. Reboiling the mother liquor recovers only 3.32 kg in a second strike and 1.33 kg in a third, taking cumulative recovery to 74.4 kg, or 94.1%, at the cost of two further boils. The paper also derives the sap-to-syrup rule from mass balance: litres of sap per litre of syrup equals 87.1 divided by the sap sugar percentage, so 2.0% sap requires 43.6 litres per litre of syrup. All separation equipment figures are SIMULATED, NOT MEASURED.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24115259

A wick selection rule for tallow and beeswax candles

The result. A candle fails in two opposite ways. If the wick is too small for the candle, its flame melts only a narrow well in the centre, the walls never melt, and the flame sinks into a tunnel of its own making. If the wick is too large, its flame melts a pool wider than the candle, the liquid overflows, the flame is fed faster than it can burn cleanly, and the candle smokes and drips. This paper states a heat-balance model that predicts melt-pool diameter from wick burn rate and wax properties, converts that prediction into a selection rule with computed failure bounds on both sides, and tabulates the rule for tallow and for beeswax. The energy needed to melt a kilogram of wax, at 20 °C ambient, is 234.0 kJ/kg for tallow and 280.0 kJ/kg for beeswax, a ratio of 1.197. At the same wick class, W3 at 7.0 g/h, the predicted pool is 36.9 mm in tallow and 29.8 mm in beeswax, a reduction of 19.2%. The practical consequence is that a wick chart for one wax does not transfer to the other: a 50 mm candle is correctly served by W4 in tallow but needs W5 in beeswax, and candles of 20 mm in tallow and 80 mm in either wax fall outside the five classes considered altogether. All results are model calculations. SIMULATED, NOT MEASURED.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24115278

Light per kilogram: candles against oil lamps

The result. Households that buy or make fuel for light face a simple question that is rarely answered in comparable units: how much light does a kilogram of fuel deliver? This paper computes total light delivered per kilogram for three sources — a tallow candle, a beeswax candle, and a simple open-wick oil lamp burning vegetable oil — from stated burn rates and stated luminous flux assumptions for each flame. The results are 1500.0 lumen-hours per kilogram for the tallow candle, 2000.0 lumen-hours per kilogram for the beeswax candle, and 3333.3 lumen-hours per kilogram for the oil lamp. For a stated evening of light, defined as 100 lumens of source flux for 4 hours, or 400.0 lumen-hours, the fuel required is 266.7 g of tallow candle, 200.0 g of beeswax candle, or 120.0 g of vegetable oil. The oil lamp leads because its larger flame produces more flux per gram burned, not because its fuel contains more energy. Its lead is not fragile: its assumed flux of 40.0 lumens would have to fall to 24.0 lumens, a drop of 40.0%, before it merely tied the beeswax candle. All results are calculations from stated assumptions. SIMULATED, NOT MEASURED.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24115304

The burn-time law for candles

The result. Two rules of thumb about candle burn time are widely repeated: that a candle gives so many hours per inch of height, implying burn time is proportional to height at fixed diameter, and that doubling the diameter doubles the burn time. This paper derives burn duration from a melt-and-consumption model and shows that neither rule survives in its simple form. In the model, burn rate grows as diameter to the power 1.5, so burn time at fixed height grows only as the square root of diameter: doubling diameter multiplies burn time by 1.414, not by 2. For beeswax at 100 mm height, burn times are 18.8 hours at 25 mm diameter, 26.5 hours at 50 mm, and 37.5 hours at 100 mm. Height scaling is not proportional either, because a 10 mm stub is left unburned: at 50 mm diameter, doubling height from 50 mm to 100 mm multiplies beeswax burn time by 2.25, from 11.8 hours to 26.5 hours, while hours per inch rise from 5.99 to 6.74. Draught and warmth shorten life through dripping loss; in a strong draught at 32 °C the waste fraction reaches 0.326 and beeswax burn time falls by 30.5%. The paper also gives production arithmetic for dipped tapers: 25 dips at 0.35 mm per dip build a 200 mm taper to 20.5 mm diameter, using 62.0 g of beeswax, at 86.4 candles per hour of hands-on dipping labour. All results are model calculations. SIMULATED, NOT MEASURED.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24115322

The strength ceiling of household vinegar

The result. Household vinegar is often described as if its strength were a matter of patience: leave wine exposed to air long enough and it will become as strong as the maker wishes. Stoichiometry sets a firmer limit. This paper calculates, from the oxidation of ethanol to acetic acid, the theoretical and practical acetic-acid concentration obtainable from starting liquids containing 5–12% alcohol by volume, after allowing for evaporation, over-oxidation, and incomplete conversion. At a combined retention of 0.8035, a 5% cider yields a practical vinegar of 4.13% acetic acid by weight per volume, and a 12% wine yields 9.92%, against a theoretical 12.34% for the wine. A starting liquid of at least 6.05% alcohol is required for a practical 5.00% vinegar. A surface-area-to-volume rule for acetification time is derived, in which time is inversely proportional to specific surface; under the stated calibration a 20-litre barrel requires 42.0 days, a wide tray 14.8 days, and a trickle system over packing 2.7 days. Finally, a pickling bound is derived for the dilution that occurs when vinegar meets the water held in vegetables. For 1 kg of vegetables covered with only 0.5 L of vinegar, the starting vinegar must be at least 6.75% to leave an equilibrated pickle at 2.5%; a nominal 5% vinegar leaves only 1.85% in that case, and does not satisfy the stated bound. All results are model calculations. SIMULATED, NOT MEASURED.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24115340

Starch recovery, stage by stage

The result. Wet extraction of starch looks simple — grind, wash, settle, dry — yet a large share of the starch in the feedstock never reaches the bag. This paper models cassava and maize extraction as a chain of four stages, each with a stated recovery fraction, and multiplies the chain to obtain overall yield per 100 kg of feedstock. Fresh cassava containing 25.0 kg of starch yields 18.44 kg of dry starch, or 20.95 kg of finished starch at 12% moisture, an overall recovery of 0.7375 of the starch present. Maize grain containing 62.0 kg of starch yields 46.09 kg dry, or 52.37 kg finished, a recovery of 0.7434. The largest single loss for cassava is washing and extraction, 2.88 kg; for maize it is steeping and washing, 9.02 kg. Water use is 40.6 litres per kilogram of finished cassava starch and 11.8 litres per kilogram of finished maize starch under the stated water inputs. Drying requires a fuel heat input of 2.67 MJ per kilogram of finished cassava starch and 2.30 MJ per kilogram for maize at 55% dryer efficiency. One concrete change is quantified for cassava: counter-current wash-water reuse combined with extended settling raises finished yield to 22.87 kg per 100 kg, a gain of 1.92 kg or 9.2%, while cutting fresh water per kilogram of finished starch from 40.6 litres to 24.0 litres. All results are model calculations. SIMULATED, NOT MEASURED.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24115349

More dye does not mean proportionally deeper colour

The result. A common household assumption is that doubling the dye in a bath doubles the depth of colour on the fibre. For madder on alum-mordanted wool, a saturation model shows that this assumption fails, and by how much. Dye uptake is modelled with a Langmuir-type isotherm, saturation capacity 90 g of dyestuff equivalent per kilogram of fibre and affinity constant 0.45 L/g, combined with a bath mass balance at a liquor ratio of 20 L per kilogram. Uptake rises from 28.94 g/kg at a dose of 50 g/kg to 48.37 g/kg at 100 g/kg, 67.41 g/kg at 200 g/kg, and only 79.05 g/kg at 400 g/kg. Doubling the dose from 200 g/kg to 400 g/kg buys a depth gain of 17.3%; doubling from 400 g/kg to 800 g/kg buys 7.2%, while bath exhaustion falls to 10.6%. A linear prediction calibrated at 50 g/kg overstates uptake at 800 g/kg by a factor of 5.46. Liquor ratio matters at fixed dose: at 100 g/kg, uptake is 58.58 g/kg at a liquor ratio of 10, 48.37 g/kg at 20, and 37.24 g/kg at 40. Mordant arithmetic is set out: alum at 20% on weight of fibre is 200 g per kilogram of wool, and raising alum from 20% to 25% raises modelled uptake at a 100 g/kg dose only from 48.37 g/kg to 49.11 g/kg. Colour yield per kilogram of dyestuff is tabulated so a household can price a shade; at an illustrative root price of 18 per kilogram, dyeing 1 kg of wool costs 1.8 in dyestuff at the 100 g/kg dose and 7.2 at the 400 g/kg dose, for a depth gain of less than two thirds. All results are model calculations. SIMULATED, NOT MEASURED.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24115364

The fuel bound of a small lime kiln, and a weighing test for completeness

The result. Pure calcium carbonate requires 1.78 MJ per kilogram of stone, or 3.178 MJ per kilogram of quicklime. With air-dry wood at 15.0 MJ/kg and stated efficiencies of 0.15 for a clamp or pit kiln and 0.30 for a small shaft kiln, the fuel bound is 1.412 kg of wood per kilogram of quicklime in the clamp and 0.706 kg in the shaft kiln. A 100 kg charge of pure stone yields 56.03 kg of quicklime and needs 79.13 kg of wood in a clamp or 39.56 kg in a shaft kiln. Because pure CaCO3 loses 43.97% of its mass as CO2, weighing stone in and product out bounds the uncalcined fraction through x = (1 − m_out/m_in)/(p × 0.4397), where p is purity — a completeness check needing only a scale. The claim that any pale rock that looks like limestone makes good lime does not survive a numerical test: a stone with 80% CaCO3, 10% silica and 10% inert material gives only 0.355 kg of free CaO per kilogram of stone against 0.560 kg for pure limestone, and pure dolomite gives only 0.304 kg of CaO per kilogram of stone while raising wood per kilogram of CaO by 68.4%.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24115380

What a household charcoal filter can and cannot do

The result. This paper sizes a household charcoal filter for a representative organic contaminant, phenol at 5.0 mg/L, from stated adsorption capacities and a contact-time model, and then applies the same arithmetic to two claims the filter cannot meet. For granular activated carbon at a stated capacity of 50 mg/g, usable volume before breakthrough at a utilisation factor of 0.50 is 5000.0 litres per kilogram; for plain wood charcoal at 10 mg/g it is 1000.0 litres per kilogram. A household using 20.0 litres per day needs, over 90 days (1800.0 litres), 0.36 kg of activated carbon or 1.8 kg of plain charcoal on capacity alone, or 0.72 kg and 3.6 kg with a factor of two for safety. Contact time sets flow: a 2.0 kg bed occupies 5.0 litres and, at a required empty-bed contact time of 10 minutes, may run at no more than 0.5 litres per minute. Salt removal fails by orders of magnitude: even at a generous capacity of 2.0 mg/g, 1 kg is exhausted by 4.0 litres at 500 mg/L salinity, and 90 days of salt removal would need 450.0 kg. Disinfection fails by mechanism: adsorption micropores are about 250 times narrower than a small bacterium, flow channels are far wider than bacteria, and a plain bed has no inactivation mechanism, so credited log removal is 0. Within its bound — taste, odour, free chlorine and many dissolved organics, for a stated volume at a slow flow — the filter is useful. It is not a desalinator and not a disinfecting device.

Lime and Ash: What a Wood-Ash Pozzolan Mortar Can and Cannot Do

The result. Per 100 kg, wood ash carries only 3.0 kg of reactive silica against rice husk ash's 77.44 kg. The result. Seeing clearly without money to spare: lenses, frames, print and light, and the tolerances that decide whether a cheap pair works.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24119325

Reading Additions Derived, Not Guessed: An Age-and-Distance Table from Hofstetter's Amplitude with Half Held in Reserve

The result. Ready-made reading spectacles are sold by age-banded guesses, and the guesses are rarely shown. This paper derives the banding from a published empirical relation - Hofstetter's average amplitude of accommodation, 18.5 minus 0.30 times age, in dioptres - combined with the stated working rule that half the amplitude is held in reserve, and computes the reading addition for ages 40 to 65 at three working distances. At 40 cm the first addition appears at age 50 in this tabulation, reaches 0.75 D at age 50 and 2.25 D at age 60; a 33 cm working distance demands about half a dioptre more at every age, and 50 cm about half a dioptre less.

The result. Variable-focus spectacles, in which the wearer dials a power rather than wearing a fixed lens, are repeatedly proposed for settings without refractionists.
