For a stated population model of spherical equivalent (mean -0.50 D, standard deviation 2.00 D), covering the central 95 percent of wearers requires a span of 7.84 D, from -4.42 to 3.42 D; the central 99 percent requires 10.3 D.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24119368

The Axis Budget: How Many Degrees of Cylinder Misalignment Each Cylinder Power Can Afford

The result. When a cylindrical lens is rotated away from its prescribed axis, it does not simply weaken: it leaves a new, oblique astigmatism whose size is twice the cylinder power times the sine of the axis error. This paper turns that standard vector result into a fitting tolerance table for workshops without lensmeter verification at every step. Holding residual astigmatism to a stated 0.25 D requires the axis of a 1.00 D cylinder to be set within 7.2 degrees, a 2.00 D cylinder within 3.6 degrees, and a 3.00 D cylinder within 2.4 degrees.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24119398

The result. Lenses marketed as blocking 95 percent of blue light are sold with the implication that they remove most of the light burden from screens. The implication does not survive band arithmetic. Under stated photopic weights for a daylight-like spectrum, the 400-450 nm band that such lenses target carries only 6.0 percent of the light the visual system actually uses; removing 95 percent of it reduces total photopic light by just 5.7 percent - the equivalent of a barely perceptible neutral dimming, leaving 94.3 percent of the light, and of any brightness-driven strain mechanism, untouched.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24119429

The result. Schools and clinics with small rooms routinely test acuity at three or four metres with charts designed for six, often without adjusting the result. This paper computes what that shortcut costs and the two corrections it requires. Geometrically, a 6 m chart used at 3 m magnifies every letter by a factor of two in angular terms, so a line recorded as 6/12 is truly 6/24 unless the distance fraction is applied; at 4 m the same line is 6/18.

The result. Children's spectacles are usually replaced because the prescription changed, but a quieter expiry runs underneath: the child's face grows, the pupils separate, and a frame centred for last year's face progressively decentres its lenses. This paper computes fit life from a stated pupillary-distance growth model and the decentration tolerance used in dispensing. Dispensed at age 6, a fixed frame stays within a plus-or-minus 2 mm centration tolerance for about 18 months of growth; a frame designed with a plus-or-minus 5 mm usable centre-distance range - sliding bridge, adjustable pads, spring temples - stays within tolerance for about 50 months.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24119461

How Large Is Large Print? A Fluent-Reading Specification Labelled by the Acuity It Serves

The result. 'Large print' is sold as a point size with no stated reader in mind, and much of it is not large enough for the readers it names. This paper derives the print specification from letter geometry and the established finding that fluent reading requires print about three times the reader's acuity threshold - the critical print size. At a 40 cm reading distance, a reader with 6/18 acuity needs capitals of about 5.6 mm, roughly 22 point; the common 16-to-18-point 'large print' label serves that reader only marginally, and a reader with 6/60 acuity needs about 74 point, capitals of 18.4 mm, at which a standard page width holds only about 17 characters per line.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24119486

The result. Minus lenses get heavy at their edges, and the edge is bought by diameter. This paper computes sagitta, edge thickness, and mass for minus lenses of -2, -4, and -6 D cut to diameters from 50 to 70 mm, from stated surface geometry for a refractive index of 1.50 and a 1.0 mm centre. A -6 D lens at 70 mm has an edge 8.71 mm thick and a mass of 24.7 g; the same lens cut to 55 mm has an edge 5.67 mm thick and a mass of 10.5 g, a saving of 57.5 percent, while the equivalent saving at -2 D is only 51.3 percent.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24119506

The Keyhole Limit: Magnifier Power, the Shrinking Window, and the Strongest Lens That Still Lets a Sentence Be Read

The result. A hand magnifier trades the two things a reader wants against each other: stronger lenses enlarge print more but show less of the page at once. This paper computes that trade-off for a stated geometric model of a 50 mm diameter simple magnifier and a stated word footprint of 12 mm, and finds where fluency breaks. Magnification rises linearly with power, from 1.0 times at +4 D to 6.0 times at +24 D, while the visible field falls in inverse proportion, from 50.0 mm to 8.3 mm.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24119524

A Millimetre Is Not a Formality: Pupillary-Distance Tolerance by Lens Power, from Prentice's Rule

The result. Pupillary distance is the cheapest measurement in dispensing and one of the most consequential, because a lens centred away from the pupil acts as a prism. By Prentice's rule, prism equals decentration in centimetres times lens power, and this paper converts that rule into a tolerance table for low-cost dispensing, where pupillary distance is often estimated or taken from a ready-made pair's fixed geometry. Against a stated binocular comfort bound of 0.67 prism dioptres, the largest permissible total pupillary-distance error falls from 6.7 mm at 1 D to 1.68 mm at 4 D and 0.84 mm at 8 D.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24119542

The result. Phone text settings are offered as small, medium, and large, which tells a low-vision user nothing. A user with 6/12 acuity needs a 0.87 mm letter at 30 cm - 10 pixels on a 300-per-inch display - and a comfortable reading size, at twice threshold, of 1.74 mm.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24119563

The result. Pinhole spectacles are sold in fixed apertures, often with the suggestion that a smaller hole gives sharper sight. That suggestion does not survive a standard blur calculation. For an eye with defocus, geometric blur grows in proportion to aperture while diffraction blur grows as aperture shrinks; combining the two at 550 nm gives, for 2 D of defocus, a minimum total blur of 7.96 minutes of arc at an aperture of 0.82 mm - an acuity near 20/159, and 3.46 times sharper than the naked 4 mm pupil, though still well short of a proper lens.

The result. A brighter lamp is the cheapest vision aid in the world, but advice about reading light is usually given in watts and adjectives. This paper replaces both with a stated model relating the eye acuity threshold to illuminance for three ages, and computes the light each age needs for fluent reading of four print sizes. Under the model, N12 large print needs about 245 lux at every age tabulated, and N18 needs only about 24 lux, while fluent N8 newspaper print needs about 3162 lux - bright task lighting - at ages 30 and 55, and at 75 is not reachable by light alone at all, because the stated acuity floor of 0.18 logMAR sits above the print demand of 0.10 logMAR.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24119594

Four Centuries, One Constant: The Resolution Bound of the Scheiner-Disc Self-Refractor

The result. The Scheiner disc - two pinholes before the eye, through which a point of light appears double whenever focus is imperfect - is the oldest self-refraction principle in optics, described in 1619, and periodically reinvented as a low-cost refractor. This paper computes its resolution bound: the smallest defocus a user can detect, as a function of pinhole separation, against a stated criterion that a doubling separation of 2 minutes of arc is reliably seen. At a 2 mm separation the bound is 0.51 D; at 3 mm it is 0.34 D; at the largest separation a pupil admits, the principle cannot resolve the quarter-dioptre step that dispensing requires.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24119608

The Step Is the Accuracy: How Trial-Lens Step Size Caps Self-Selected Correction, and a Two-Stage Kit That Beats It

The result. Where no refractionist is available, wearers can be offered trial lenses and asked which is clearer - but the fineness of the set decides in advance how good the result can be. This paper computes the ceiling that the step size imposes. A set in 1.00 D steps leaves a worst-case residual error of 0.5 D, costing about 0.2 logMAR of acuity by a stated clinical rule of thumb; a 0.50 D set halves that to 0.25 D and 0.1 logMAR, at 25 lenses per eye form over a plus-or-minus 6 D range; a 0.25 D set reaches dispensing-grade worst case 0.125 D but doubles the swapping again to about 8.0 minutes of comparisons, past the point where a wearer's judgements stay reliable.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24119616

Drawing the Shelf from the Distribution: The Smallest Stock-Lens Inventory That Serves a Stated Share of Wearers Within a Quarter Dioptre

The result. Ready-made and stock-lens services fail quietly at the ordering stage: the powers on the shelf decide in advance which fraction of a population can be served. This paper computes that fraction for a stated population model - spherical equivalent normally distributed with mean -0.50 D and standard deviation 2.00 D - and finds the smallest inventories that serve stated shares of wearers within 0.25 D of their true power. A range of -6 to +4 D in 0.50 D steps (21 powers) serves 98.92 percent within tolerance; halving the step to 0.25 D doubles the stock lines while adding only 0.0 percentage points, because at a 0.25 D tolerance adjacent quarter-step intervals already overlap the tolerance band.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24119623

The Prescription Is a Place: How Vertex Distance Rewrites Strong Lenses, and the Power at Which It Starts to Matter

The result. A lens's power is not a property of the lens alone: it is the power at a stated distance from the eye, and moving the lens changes what the eye receives. This paper computes the effective power change when spectacles sit at a different vertex distance from the one used at refraction, for powers from 4 to 14 D in both signs, using the standard vertex formula. The changes are negligible below about 6 D, reach a quarter dioptre for a 4 mm shift at about 7.2 D, and exceed half a dioptre at the highest powers for the shifts that ordinary frame fit produces - long lashes, deep-set eyes, a frame worn low.

Teeth cleaned, dosed and treated with what is at hand: brushes, pastes, rinses and the small clinic, each priced per person.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24119137

When a Toothbrush Stops Cleaning: A Splay-Based Replacement Rule and Its Cost per Effective Brush

The result. Toothbrush replacement is usually governed by habit or by a calendar printed on packaging, not by how well the brush still cleans. This study models plaque-removal efficacy as falling linearly by 0.045 per week of twice-daily use from an initial index of 1.00, and computes end-of-life and mean efficacy, brushes per year, and annual cost at a bulk price of USD 0.60 for replacement intervals from 2 to 16 weeks. Efficacy first falls below 0.70 at 6.67 weeks. The longest interval whose mean efficacy over the brush's life stays at or above 0.85 is 6 weeks, using 8.67 brushes a year at USD 5.20. An 8-week interval lowers the cost to USD 3.90 with a mean efficacy of 0.82, while a 12-week interval gives a mean efficacy of only 0.73 at USD 2.60. The recommended rule is replacement at about six weeks on performance grounds, with eight weeks as a stated budget compromise. This study is simulated, not measured. SIMULATED, NOT MEASURED.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24119153

A Smear, a Pea, or a Ribbon: Fluoride Dose per Brushing for Young Children and the Dispensing Rule That Keeps It in Range

The result. Advice on how much toothpaste a young child should use is given in pictures, a smear or a pea, without stating the dose those pictures deliver. This study computes fluoride applied and swallowed for three dispensing amounts (smear 0.10 g, pea 0.25 g, full ribbon 0.60 g), two paste strengths (1000 and 1450 ppm), and two age bands, with stated swallowed fractions and a stated upper intake criterion of 0.10 mg per kg per day. For ages two to three at 1450 ppm, a ribbon applies 0.87 mg per brushing and brings total daily intake to 0.822 mg, 68.5 percent of the upper criterion, while a smear of 1000 ppm paste brings it to only 0.36 mg, 30.0 percent. For ages four to five a pea of 1450 ppm paste totals 0.409 mg per day, 22.7 percent of the criterion. The dispensing rule that follows is a smear for ages two to three and a pea for ages four to five. This study is simulated, not measured. SIMULATED, NOT MEASURED.

How Much Floss Is Enough: The Handling Length That Cleans Every Contact and the Waste in the Standard Instruction

The result. The standard floss instruction, to take about 45 cm, is tested against the geometry of the task it describes. Cleaning 26 interdental contacts with a fresh 2.5 cm segment for each, plus 8 cm for each of two hand wraps, requires 81.0 cm of floss. The standard 45 cm leaves 29.0 cm of fresh working length after the wraps, enough for 11 contacts with a fresh segment, leaving 15 contacts to be cleaned with a reused segment or not cleaned. Annual cost at the required length is USD 5.91 for daily use at a stated price of USD 0.02 per metre. A tied loop or a holder, which removes the wrap length, is identified as the practical route to complete cleaning. The standard instruction, as written, does not deliver the complete cleaning it promises. This study is simulated, not measured. SIMULATED, NOT MEASURED.

Brushing with a Cup Instead of a Running Tap: Water Saved per Person and the Rinse Volume That Still Clears Paste

The result. Toothbrushing advice assumes a running tap and never prices the water. This study compares a running-tap routine (6 litres per minute for 45 seconds, 4.5 litres per brushing) with a single 200 mL cup, and checks the cup against a stated dilution model of paste clearance in which the residual fraction after rinsing with volume V is exp(-V/60). The cup saves 4.3 litres per brushing, 3139.0 litres per person per year at two brushings daily, and 15695 litres per year for a household of five. The volume needed to reach a 5 percent residual is 179.7 mL, and a 200 mL cup leaves a residual of 3.57 percent. The cup method is a large saving with no modelled hygiene penalty. This study is simulated, not measured. SIMULATED, NOT MEASURED.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24119193

Sizing Interdental Brushes by Passage Diameter: A Selection Rule That Covers the Spaces a Single Size Misses

The result. Interdental brushes are commonly supplied in a single size, as if all interdental spaces were alike. This study models 26 spaces with stated passage diameters (eight at 0.6 mm, eight at 0.8 mm, six at 1.0 mm, four at 1.3 mm) and a stated fit rule under which a brush cleans a space when its diameter is at least the passage diameter and at most 0.4 mm larger. No single size cleans more than 61.5 percent of the spaces; the best single size, 0.8 mm, covers 16 of 26. The smallest set covering all 26 spaces contains two sizes, 0.8 and 1.3 mm. The sizing rule that follows is to gauge the tightest and widest spaces and stock the two sizes that span them. This study is simulated, not measured. SIMULATED, NOT MEASURED.

Miswak or Manufactured Brush: Cost per Year of Effective Cleaning Where Brushes and Paste Are Hard to Afford

The result. Miswak, the chewing stick of Salvadora persica, is widely described as the economical alternative to a manufactured brush and paste. This study tests that description at stated prices: a stick at USD 0.15 replaced weekly, against a brush at USD 0.60 replaced every eight weeks plus toothpaste at USD 0.80 per 100 g tube consumed in 100 days. A year of purchased miswak costs USD 7.80; a year of brush and paste costs USD 6.82. Dividing by stated relative efficacy (brush and paste 1.00, miswak five times daily 0.92, miswak twice daily 0.78) gives costs per unit of efficacy of USD 6.82, USD 8.48, and USD 10.00 respectively. Purchased miswak is therefore not the cheaper system; it is cheaper only where sticks are gathered at no cash cost. This study is simulated, not measured. SIMULATED, NOT MEASURED.

One Varnish Vial, How Many Children: The Dose per Child That Covers the Teeth Without Emptying the Vial Early

The result. Fluoride varnish in school programmes is usually dispensed as one unit-dose vial per child, so the package sets the dose. At a pooled dose of 0.25 mL, ten vials serve 16 children and apply 4.80 mg each, still above the modelled coating need after waste. Dose should be set by the dentition, not by the vial. A Level Teaspoon in a Cup of Water: The Actual Salt Concentration of the Standard Aftercare Rinse and a More Repeatable Recipe

The result. Patients recovering from a tooth extraction are told to rinse with a teaspoon of salt in a cup of warm water, an instruction whose terms are undefined. This study computes the concentration of six common versions using stated spoon masses (half teaspoon 3.0 g, level teaspoon 6.0 g, heaped teaspoon 9.0 g) and vessel volumes of 200, 250, and 350 mL, against the isotonic reference of 0.9 percent and a stated tolerable band of 0.7 to 1.2 percent. The six versions produce 1.2, 2.4, 3.0, 3.6, 1.71, and 1.5 percent; only one, the half teaspoon in 250 mL at 1.2 percent, falls inside the band. Steam Sterilisation for a Small Dental Clinic: Cycle Capacity, Daily Throughput, and the Instrument Sets That Remove the Bottleneck

The result. In a small dental clinic the steriliser, not the clinician, can set the pace of the day. This study models a benchtop steam steriliser holding six instrument sets per 45-minute cycle across an eight-hour day, and derives both daily capacity and the stock of sets a clinic must own to keep chairs working. Capacity is ten cycles and 60 sets per day, which meets demand at four, six, and eight patients per hour. The binding constraint is stock: the rotation rule of chamber load plus sets in use during one cycle plus two in preparation gives 13 complete sets at six patients per hour and 14 at eight. Light for Looking in Mouths: Illuminance at Working Distance, Battery Life, and the Cheapest Headlamp Class That Meets an Examination Threshold

The result. Examiners working without a dental operating light rely on headlamps sold by lumen ratings that do not state the illuminance at the mouth. This study converts three lamp classes (100, 200, and 400 lumens, priced at USD 6, 12, and 25 as stated values) into illuminance at a 0.35 m working distance, assuming the output is focused into a 0.20 m spot with a stated optical efficiency of 0.70, and compares the result with a stated examination threshold of 10,000 lux. The classes deliver 2228, 4456, and 8913 lux respectively: none meets the threshold. Meeting it in the same spot requires about 449 lumens delivered to that spot. Lamp purchases for examination work should therefore be specified by spot illuminance at working distance, not by lumens. This study is simulated, not measured. SIMULATED, NOT MEASURED.

Hand-Mixed or Capsule: Glass Ionomer Waste and Restorations per Kit in Atraumatic Restorative Treatment

The result. Atraumatic restorative treatment depends on glass ionomer, bought either as powder and liquid for hand mixing or as pre-dosed capsules. This study compares the two routes at stated values: a restoration needs 0.20 g of mixed material; hand mixing prepares 0.35 g per attempt with 12 percent of mixes discarded for ratio failure; a capsule delivers 0.40 g with 2 percent failure. A 50 g hand-mix kit at USD 28 yields about 126 successful restorations at USD 0.22 each, while a box of 50 capsules at USD 42 yields 49 at USD 0.86 each. Hand mixing wastes 42.9 percent of its material as over-mix. Capsules buy consistency, not economy. This study is simulated, not measured. SIMULATED, NOT MEASURED.

The result. Denture cleansing tablets are used one per soak by label instruction, and halving them is a common household economy of unknown effect. This study evaluates dose and soak time together under a stated concentration-time criterion of 3.0 normalised units, where a whole tablet in 150 mL defines a concentration of 1.00 and a half tablet 0.50. A whole tablet reaches the criterion at 30 minutes (3.0 units); a half tablet reaches it at 60 minutes (3.0 units) and exceeds it eightfold overnight at 480 minutes (24.0 units). Halving the tablet for overnight soaking saves USD 21.90 per wearer per year at a stated tablet price of USD 0.12. This study is simulated, not measured. SIMULATED, NOT MEASURED.

What Stays in the Tube: Residual Toothpaste by Tube Type and the Annual Loss for a Family of Five

The result. Every toothpaste tube is discarded with paste still inside. This study quantifies the residue for a family of five using 5.0 g per day, at stated residuals of 8 g for a standard crimped tube emptied by hand, 3 g with a rolling key, and 2 g for a stand-up pump, from a nominal 100 g tube priced at USD 0.80. The standard tube gives 19.84 tubes per year and loses 158.7 g of paste annually inside discarded tubes, worth USD 1.27; the rolling key cuts the loss to 56.4 g and saves USD 0.82 per year in purchases. Drying Matters More Than Disinfectant: Spacing and Airflow Rules for Storing Forty Toothbrushes in a Classroom

The result. School toothbrushing programmes store dozens of brushes between sessions, and guidance often reaches for disinfectant soaking where the underlying problem is that brushes stay wet and touching. This study models drying under stated assumptions: a shaken brush head carries 2.0 mL of water, evaporation proceeds at 0.020 mL per minute with heads separated and at half that rate with heads touching, and the dry target is a residual of 0.2 mL. Separated brushes reach the target in 90.0 minutes; touching brushes need 180.0 minutes. A four-row rack at 25 mm spacing stores forty brushes in rows 250 mm long. Spacing, a one-time construction decision, replaces a recurring disinfectant cost. This study is simulated, not measured. SIMULATED, NOT MEASURED.

A Day at the Mobile Dental Clinic: Appointment Length, Turnaround, and the Schedule That Treats the Most Patients Without Rushing Treatment

The result. A mobile dental clinic's scarce asset is chair time in a community it may not revisit for months. This study builds the clinic day from stated values: 420 minutes of chair time, treatment times of 20, 30, and 25 minutes for examination with cleaning, filling, and extraction, a case mix of 50, 30, and 20 percent giving a weighted treatment time of 24.0 minutes, and turnaround of 5 minutes with parallel instrument processing against 12 minutes serial. Parallel turnaround treats 14 patients in the day against 11 serial, a gain of 3 patients. At a stated 15 percent no-show rate, booking two appointments beyond capacity raises the expected number treated from 11.9 to 13.6. This study is simulated, not measured. SIMULATED, NOT MEASURED.

Charcoal Toothpaste and the Whitening Claim: Abrasive Wear per Shade Gained and the Fluoride the Claim Leaves Out

The result. Charcoal toothpastes are sold on a whitening claim. This study tests the claim against a stated saturating model of stain removal, in which any paste above an abrasivity index of 60 reaches the same shade gain of 1.90 shades at four weeks, and a stated wear model in which annual abrasive wear is proportional to the abrasivity index. At stated indices of 120 for a charcoal paste, 70 for a standard fluoride paste, and 90 for a peroxide whitening paste, all three reach the same 1.90 shade gain, while the charcoal paste's annual wear is 87600 normalised units against 51100 for the standard paste, an excess of 71.0 percent. The model: designs for licence

Aldred Industries does not manufacture. It solves the problem and publishes the design; the publication, with its DOI and its date, is the public record of the work. Manufacturers license the designs and build them. Any design catalogued on this page is available for licence to manufacturers from Aldred Industries.

Bait Stations Along an Ant Trail: The Spacing at Which Discovery Time, Not Dose, Becomes the Limit

The result. Place liquid bait stations about 2 m apart along the active trail (nine stations for an 18 m trail, about USD 9.90 in hardware), refill weekly, do not spray the trail during baiting, and allow ten days before judging the attempt; on the stated assumptions the colony dose is complete in about 2.8 days at this spacing, and wider spacings fail mainly by delaying discovery.

The Thick Mattress Problem: Heat-Soak Time Scales With the Square of Thickness in Bed Bug Room Treatments

The result. Plan the hold from the thickest item: at 60 C air, allow at least 4 hours for 10 cm of foam, 16 hours for 20 cm, and treat 30 cm items (36 hours) as impractical to clear in place — split, open or remove thick items so no conduction path exceeds about 10 cm, and verify with a probe in the core of the thickest remaining item.

The result. Hang two pheromone traps per small textile store room, at low level and at opposite ends, replace them every eight weeks (USD 12 per round), and treat any catch as a trigger to inspect and treat the textiles themselves; a third trap in the same room adds cost without detection.

Wash bedding intended as a mite control measure at 60 C with a normal-length cycle (about 24 minutes of allowance covers the 3-log target at the load's coldest point); treat 40 C washing as allergen-removal cleaning only, and use hot tumble drying, freezing or encasements for items that cannot be washed at 60 C.

The result. Space snap traps no more than 12 m apart along a confirmed mouse run, concentrate them at wall junctions, corners and the gaps behind appliances, and check and reset them every night; on the stated assumptions this gives a 0.995 capture probability within 48 hours for a single mouse, and extra traps should be justified as redundancy, not coverage.

The result. Four Pages at a Time: An Imposition Bound for A5 Booklets on A4 and A3 Sheets That Minimises Trim and Signature Waste

The result. An A5 booklet must contain a multiple of four pages: a 50-page text becomes a 52-page booklet with 2 blank pages (3.8 percent waste), a 32-page booklet needs exactly 8 A3 sheets, and at the stated sheet prices paper cost per booklet is identical on A3 and A4 stock, so the stock choice should be made on equipment and labour, not paper price.

The result. When Duplex Printing Saves Nothing: Reprint Losses, Jam Rates and the Net Paper Saving From Printing on Both Sides

The result. Duplex printing saves 49.8 percent of sheets with no user errors, 47.3 percent at a 5 percent reprint rate and 39.8 percent even at 20 percent errors; the saving is not exhausted until the reprint error fraction reaches 0.998, so duplex should be mandated for standard long documents and management effort spent on templates rather than on policing short jobs.

The result. Nesting rectangular envelope blanks on a 650x900 mm sheet gives 6 DL blanks per sheet at 24.5 percent waste, 4 C5 blanks at 36.0 percent waste, and 9 C6 blanks at 13.8 percent waste, so C6 is the format a small cutter should standardise on for this sheet size, with the other formats quoted at their true waste penalty.

Locks, Fences, Gates and Security — 17 papers

The house defended at its edges: locks, bolts, fences and gates, each tested against the force or the search it actually faces.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24121551

Euro Cylinder Protrusion: Testing the Three-Millimetre Rule Against a Wrench Torque Model

The result. Euro-profile cylinders that stand proud of the door furniture can be gripped and snapped, and fitting guidance commonly allows at most 3 mm of protrusion. This study tests that rule against a stated torque model in which a wrench attack develops T = 18 + 2.6 p newton-metres at protrusion p millimetres, a standard cylinder bridge fails at 28 Nm, and a reinforced cylinder with a sacrificial cut fails at 55 Nm. The standard cylinder's margin is exhausted at a protrusion of 3.9 mm: at the customary 3 mm the attack torque is 25.8 Nm, already close to the bridge strength, while at 10 mm it reaches 44.0 Nm and the cylinder fails with room to spare. The reinforced cylinder holds its margin until 14.3 mm of protrusion. The 3 mm rule is therefore confirmed as a sensible fitting limit for standard cylinders, but it is a limit with little slack, and the calculation argues for measuring protrusion at fitting and for choosing a reinforced cylinder whenever the door and furniture combination cannot be brought under about 3 mm. SIMULATED, NOT MEASURED.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24121578

Deadbolt Throw Length: How Much Engagement Depth a Longer Bolt Actually Buys Against Frame Splitting

The result. A deadbolt is sold by its throw, the distance the bolt travels into the frame, yet what a household needs is resistance to the frame splitting around the strike. This study models that resistance from stated assumptions: a steel bolt of 11 mm by 20 mm section has a shear capacity near 61,600 N and never governs, while the timber frame around the strike pocket is modelled as failing at F = 700 + 330 e^0.8 newtons, where e is the engagement depth in millimetres after a 4 mm allowance for door gap and strike recess. A standard 25 mm throw then resists 4470 N and delays entry by about 3.99 minutes under the stated attack model, while the longest throw tabulated, 38 mm, resists 6242 N, a gain of 39.6 percent, for a delay of 5.26 minutes. Resistance reaches the 5,000 N level used here as a working target only at a throw of 28.8 mm. The practical rule is therefore to specify engagement depth, not catalogue throw, and to expect diminishing returns beyond about 32 mm because the exponent on engagement is well below one. SIMULATED, NOT MEASURED.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24121585

The Door Chain Claim Examined: Why a Chain Assembly Cannot Hold What Its Packaging Suggests

The result. Door chains are sometimes sold with language suggesting they withstand hundreds of kilograms of force. This study examines a claim of 750 kg, about 7,350 N, by computing the strength of each element of a typical chain assembly from stated assumptions: the chain itself fails in tension at 4,200 N, its keep plate bends at 1,800 N, and the anchor plate's four small screws, embedded 16 mm into the edge grain of a timber door, pull out as a system at about 676 N. An assembly fails at its weakest element, so the fitted chain resists about 676 N, which is 9.2 percent of the claimed figure. Even doubling the screw embedment to 32 mm raises the assembly only to 1351 N, at which point the bending plate governs. The claim is therefore not sustained for the assembly as fitted, whatever the chain link alone may hold on a test machine. A door chain should be understood as a device for identifying a caller while the door is ajar, not as a forced-entry barrier, and households relying on it for resistance should redirect the same money to the strike and frame. SIMULATED, NOT MEASURED.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24121591

Boundary Fence Height: Climb Delay, Cost per Metre, and the Height at Which Extra Panels Stop Paying

The result. A boundary fence delays rather than stops, and the useful question is how much delay each additional increment of height buys for its cost. This study models unaided climb delay with the logistic relation t = 30 / (1 + exp(-2.6 (h - 1.75))) seconds for height h in metres, and fence cost as USD 34 + 52 (h - 1.2)^1.25 per metre run, both stated relations. Delay rises from 5.8 seconds at 1.2 m to 16.0 seconds at 1.8 m and 25.3 seconds at 2.4 m, while cost rises from USD 34.0 to USD 99.31 per metre over the same span. Delay bought per USD 100 peaks at 2.0 m in the table. A trellis or angled topping adding a stated 9 seconds at any height improves the 1.8 m fence by 56.2 percent for USD 11 per metre, which is a better purchase than the final increment of panel height. The resulting guidance is to build to about 1.8 m where regulations allow, add a topping that is awkward to grip, and treat heights beyond about 2.2 m as spending on appearance and privacy rather than on delay. SIMULATED, NOT MEASURED.

Fence Post Spacing Under Wind Load: The Widest Bay a Timber Post Can Carry Before Bending Governs

The result. A solid garden fence is a sail, and its posts are the masts. This study computes post bending stress under a stated gust pressure of 0.55 kN per square metre on a 1.8 m solid fence, with each interior post carrying one bay and acting as a 125 mm square timber section of stated allowable bending stress 8 MPa. Stress rises in direct proportion to bay width: 4.92 MPa at the conventional 1.8 m spacing, reaching the allowable limit at a spacing of 2.92 m and 8.2 MPa at 3.0 m. Widening bays from 1.8 m to 3.0 m would save about 6.7 posts over a 30 m run, but it spends the entire structural margin to do so. The practical rule is to keep bays at or below about 2.4 m on exposed sites, where a margin of roughly a fifth remains, and to treat the common 3 m panel span as suitable only for sheltered gardens or stronger posts. SIMULATED, NOT MEASURED.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24121606

Timber Gate Sag: Diagonal Brace Angle and the Widest Gate That Still Latches After Five Summers

The result. A timber garden gate that sags drops at its latch edge until the latch misses its keep, at which point the gate stops performing any security function at all. This study models sag from stated assumptions: an unbraced ledged gate drops 4.5 W^3 millimetres at width W metres after five seasons, a correctly fitted diagonal brace removes a fraction of that drop peaking at a brace angle of 45 degrees, and a latch tolerates 10 mm of drop. Unbraced, the widest gate that still latches is 1.3 m; braced, it is 1.96 m. At the common width of 1.5 m the brace angle is 35.0 degrees, the unbraced drop is 15.2 mm, and bracing reduces it to 3.1 mm. The brace must run from the lower hinge corner toward the upper latch corner; a brace fitted the other way assists the sag it was meant to prevent. The rule is to brace every ledged gate wider than about a metre and to check the brace direction before checking anything else. SIMULATED, NOT MEASURED.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24121620

How Many Different Keys a Five-Pin Lock Really Has: Counting Usable Bitting Combinations Under an Adjacent-Cut Rule

The result. A five-pin cylinder with six depth steps per pin is often described as offering 7,776 different keys, the value of six to the fifth power. That figure counts combinations a manufacturer cannot actually cut, because a working key must satisfy the maximum adjacent cut rule: neighbouring depths that differ too steeply produce a ramp the key cannot slide past the pins. This study enumerates all 7,776 combinations exactly and tests each against a stated adjacent-cut limit of three steps. Only 4034 combinations survive, so 48.1 percent of the advertised space is unusable, and two randomly keyed locks share a key with probability 0.0248 percent. Among 200 houses keyed at random from the usable set, the expected number of pairs sharing a key is 4.93. The figures argue for reading lock specifications by their usable combinations rather than their pin count, and for preferring six-pin cylinders or additional depth steps where key uniqueness across a street or a housing development actually matters. SIMULATED, NOT MEASURED.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24121630

Worn Keypad Digits: How Visible Wear on a Code Lock Collapses Its Search Space

The result. A keypad lock's code is only as secret as its buttons are unworn. When daily pressing polishes or fades a subset of digits, an observer learns which digits the code contains, and the search space collapses. This study counts the collapse exactly, by enumerating all 10,000 four-digit codes and retaining those built only from the worn digits. If wear reveals four distinct digits, only 256 candidate codes remain, a reduction by a factor of 39.1, exhaustible in 25.6 minutes at six seconds per attempt without a lockout. Three worn digits leave 81 candidates and two worn digits leave 16. A six-digit code with four worn digits leaves 4096 candidates, or about 6.8 hours of continuous trying, which restores practical safety even when fully worn. The rules that follow are to prefer six-digit codes, to choose codes whose digits repeat so wear reveals less, to clean or replace worn keypads, and to treat a lockout feature as worth more than extra digits against a patient attacker. SIMULATED, NOT MEASURED.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24121643

Letterbox Reach: The Minimum Distance Between a Mail Slot and the Inside Latch That Defeats a Hooked Tool

The result. A mail slot through a front door is also a hole through which a hooked wire can be worked toward the inside latch, and on many doors the latch sits within easy reach of the slot. This study models the geometry from stated assumptions: a 700 mm hooked tool, after losses at the flap, the door thickness and its own bends, commands an effective radius of 520 mm from the slot centre, while an internal cover box behind the slot reduces that radius to 180 mm. A latch is taken as vulnerable when its distance from the slot is within the effective radius, and a safety margin of 80 mm is added for the design rule. On that basis an unprotected door wants at least 600 mm between slot and latch, a distance many doors, where the slot sits in the mid-rail a few hundred millimetres from the latch, do not have. With a cover box fitted, the required separation falls to 260 mm, which almost any door satisfies. The cheapest robust rule is therefore not to move the latch but to cage the slot, and to keep keys and thumbturns out of the unprotected radius in the meantime. SIMULATED, NOT MEASURED.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24121657

Motion-Activated Lighting: The Mounting Height That Covers a Driveway Without Leaving a Dark Path Beneath the Sensor

The result. A motion-activated light protects only the ground its sensor actually sees, and mounting height moves two boundaries at once: the far edge of detection, set by the sensor's 12 m slant range, and the dead zone beneath the fitting, which grows with height. This study samples a 5 m by 8 m driveway on a 0.1 m grid from stated assumptions, testing each point against the range limit and a dead zone whose radius is 0.45 times the mounting height. Coverage is highest at the lowest tabulated height and falls as the dead zone swallows the near corner: 97.5 percent at 2.5 m against 90.2 percent at 5 m, where the dead radius reaches 2.25 m and an approach hugging the wall passes beneath the sensor entirely. The practical rule balances this geometry against reach and tampering: mount the sensor between about 2.5 m and 3.5 m, above casual reach but low enough that the dead zone stays under about 1.6 m, and aim the fitting across the likely approach path rather than along the wall it is mounted on. SIMULATED, NOT MEASURED.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24121671

Padlock Shackle Diameter: The Thickness at Which Hand Bolt Cutters Run Out of Jaw Force

The result. A padlock is usually attacked through its shackle with bolt cutters, and whether the attack succeeds is a contest between the force the jaws can develop and the force the shackle's cross-section demands. This study models the demand as F = 330 d^2 newtons for shackle diameter d in millimetres, a stated relation combining hardened steel shear strength with jaw wedge action, and sets it against stated jaw capacities of 17,000 N for a compact 450 mm cutter and 38,000 N for a full-size 900 mm cutter. The compact cutter is defeated at a shackle diameter of 7.1 mm and the full-size cutter at 10.7 mm; an 8 mm shackle requires 21120 N and an 11 mm shackle 39930 N. Diameter is therefore a strong defence because demand grows with its square, but it is a defence with a ceiling, since a determined attacker can bring a larger tool. The purchasing rule is to specify a shackle of at least 10 mm in hardened steel, shrouded where possible so the jaws cannot seat, and to remember that the hasp and chain beside the lock must be graded to match. SIMULATED, NOT MEASURED.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24121683

Pool Gate Latch Height: The Placement at Which a Latch Leaves a Young Child's Reach Without Leaving an Adult's

The result. A gate between a garden and a swimming pool must be operable by every adult and by almost no young child, and the dimension that decides the matter is latch height. This study models a child's chance of reaching and operating a latch as a logistic function of height, with a midpoint of 1.38 m for five-year-olds and 1.43 m for six-year-olds, both stated values, and asks where the chance falls below one percent. At 1.35 m a five-year-old still succeeds 66.08 percent of the time; at 1.5 m the figure is 6.5 percent. The one-percent level for five-year-olds is reached at 1.587 m, and for six-year-olds at 1.637 m, which sits close to the 1.65 m level above which shorter adults begin to find a latch awkward. The window between child resistance and adult convenience is therefore narrow but real: a latch placed between about 1.60 m and 1.65 m, ideally shielded so that reach alone is not enough, satisfies both sides of the requirement on the stated model. SIMULATED, NOT MEASURED.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24121699

Sliding Door Security Bars: The Dowel Diameter at Which a Timber Bar Stops Buckling Under a Forcing Load

The result. A length of timber dropped into the track of a sliding patio door is among the cheapest security devices sold, and among the least specified: dowels are bought by eye, in diameters that vary by a factor of two. This study computes the buckling load of a 0.9 m pine dowel from the Euler relation with a stated elastic modulus of 9 GPa, and compares it with a stated forcing load of 2,600 N at which the door's rollers and track begin to deform. A 25 mm dowel buckles at 2103 N, below the forcing load, so the door's own hardware is not the weakest element; the bar is. The buckling load reaches 2,600 N at a diameter of 26.4 mm, and a 32 mm dowel carries 5645 N at a stated price of USD 12.96. Because buckling load grows with the fourth power of diameter, small increases in thickness are disproportionately effective, and the rule is to fit a bar of at least 30 mm diameter, cut to a close fit in the track so that it is loaded axially rather than bent. SIMULATED, NOT MEASURED.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24121715

The Hidden Spare Key: How Much of a Burglar's Search the Common Hiding Places Already Cover

The result. A spare key hidden outside the house is a second front door whose lock is the obscurity of its hiding place, and obscurity can be priced. This study uses a stated distribution of hiding places among households that hide a key, from 22 percent under a mat down to single-figure novelties, and models an intruder who searches places in order of popularity at 40 seconds per place. The three commonest places, mat, fake rock and plant pot, already cover 49 percent of hidden keys; the first five places cover 66 percent and take 3.33 minutes to search; eight places cover 83 percent in 5.33 minutes. Only the tenth category, genuinely original places at 10 percent, lies outside a short search. The conclusion is that a hidden key in any commonly used place should be treated as a key handed to a patient stranger, and the safe alternatives are a coded key safe, which replaces obscurity with a combination, or a key left with a person rather than an object. SIMULATED, NOT MEASURED.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24121725

Strike Plate Screws: The Length at Which a Door Strike Stops Being the Weakest Part of the Lock

The result. Many deadbolts are defeated not at the bolt but at the strike plate, whose short screws bite only into the thin frame facing rather than the structural timber behind it. This study computes strike resistance from stated assumptions: a 4 mm screw embedded in softwood resists pull-out at 35.2 N per millimetre of embedment, four screws share load with a factor of 0.7, and the frame itself splits at 5,400 N, capping the assembly. The short 19 mm screws supplied with many locks give a system resistance of only 1873 N. Resistance climbs linearly with length until, at 54.8 mm, the screws would hold as much as the frame itself can bear; the 76 mm screws tabulated reach the 5400 N cap, an improvement by a factor of 2.88 over the short screws, for a total screw cost of USD 0.48. Beyond the crossover, longer screws buy nothing because the frame becomes the failing part. The resulting rule is simple: fit strike screws of at least 65 mm that reach the structural timber, and spend any further money on the frame rather than on still longer screws. SIMULATED, NOT MEASURED.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24121742

Thorn Hedge Planting Spacing: Closing the Gaps in a Living Barrier Before Its Second Winter

The result. A thorn hedge is a fence that grows, and its weak years are its first ones, when the whips stand in a row of separate bushes with walk-through gaps between them. This study models shrub spread as r(t) = 0.18 + 0.22 t metres from stated values, and computes when neighbouring canopies meet at each planting spacing: at 0.9 m spacing the hedge closes in 1.23 years, at 1.5 m spacing it takes 2.59 years, a full extra growing season and more spent as a dotted line rather than a barrier. Crossing delay at maturity, computed from a stated stem density, is 40 seconds for a single 0.6 m row at 0.9 m spacing and 80 seconds for a double staggered row of 1.2 m depth. Planting cost at 0.9 m spacing is 12 plants per 10 m. The rule is to plant thorn hedging at 0.9 m or closer in a double staggered row where the ground allows, accepting the plant cost as the price of closing the barrier before its second winter, and to protect the young hedge with a temporary fence rather than pretending it is already a barrier. SIMULATED, NOT MEASURED.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24121748

Window Sash Lock Spacing: How Many Locks a Wide Window Needs Before Prying Flexes the Rail Open

The result. A wide sliding or casement window fitted with a single central lock can be opened by prying at a corner, flexing the meeting rail until the latch clears its keeper, without breaking anything. This study models the rail as a beam from stated values: a 700 N pry, an aluminium rail of elastic modulus 69 GPa and second moment of area 7.5e-9 m^4, and a 6 mm flex at which the latch disengages. Deflection grows with the cube of the unsupported span, reaching 6.09 mm at 0.6 m and 14.43 mm at 0.8 m, and the widest span that stays below the escape gap is 0.59 m. A window 1.8 m wide therefore needs 3 locks rather than the customary one, at a stated hardware cost of USD 27, while a 1.2 m window needs 2. The rule is to space window locks no more than about 0.55 m apart along the meeting rail, and to count a window's locks by its width rather than by habit. SIMULATED, NOT MEASURED.

Postal Services, Addresses and Deliveries — 17 papers

Mail where the network is thin: boxes, codes, rounds and relays, each sized by distance, volume and the walk home.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24121593

How Often Must an Address List Be Re-Canvassed? Household Churn and the Cost of Stale Addresses

The result. An address list begins rotting the day it is finished: households move, buildings appear and vanish, names change. This paper prices that decay against the cost of repairing it. With a stated household churn of 3 percent per month, the stale fraction of a list reaches 30.6 percent after a year untouched.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24121599

A Post Counter Inside a Village Shop: The Daily Transaction Count That Covers an Agency's Costs

The result. Where a staffed post office cannot pay, a counter inside an existing shop — an agency — is the standard alternative, but agencies are opened on enthusiasm and closed on arithmetic. This paper states the arithmetic. The agency's fixed monthly costs to the operator are USD 99.0 (retainer USD 45, equipment and training USD 22, cash float USD 14, audit visits USD 18), and each transaction earns a stated net USD 0.31.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24121603

Bicycle or Motorbike for the Rural Round? The Daily Distance at Which the Faster Machine Starts Paying

The result. Whether a rural postman should ride a bicycle or a motorbike is usually argued from purchase price, but the honest comparison is annual: the motorbike carries a fixed annual penalty of USD 370.0 in depreciation, maintenance and insurance, and repays it only through the rider's time, valued here at USD 1.50 per hour, saved by travelling at 28 rather than 12 km/h. The repayment is complete at a crossover round distance of 31.4 km per day. At 20 km per day the bicycle still wins, costing USD 758.33 a year against USD 892.19 — a difference of USD 133.86; by 40 km per day the motorbike is already ahead, and at 80 km per day it is ahead by USD 574.57.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24121618

Spacing Communal Letter Boxes Along a Road: The Interval That Balances Walking Against Collection Cost

The result. Where door-to-door delivery is unaffordable, households collect mail from communal box clusters along the road, and someone must choose how far apart the clusters stand. This paper balances the two costs that spacing controls: each cluster carries a stated annual cost of USD 180 (bank, shelter and collection stop), while households walk an average of a quarter of the spacing and value that walking at USD 6 per household-kilometre per year in aggregate. At a density of 80 households per kilometre the balance falls at a spacing of 1.225 km, a mean walk of 0.306 km, and a combined cost of USD 293.94 per kilometre of road per year.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24121628

Collect the Roadside Box on a Schedule or When It Is Full? A Fill-Trigger Rule and Its Overflow Risk

The result. Posting boxes are emptied on timetables written for the average box, though boxes fill at wildly different rates. This paper compares a fixed collection schedule with a fill trigger — empty the box when it holds a stated number of items, reported by sensor or by the deliverer's count. At a stated arrival rate of 26 items a day, trip cost USD 7.50 and delay priced at USD 0.03 per item-day, the best fixed schedule collects every 4.39 days at USD 3.421 a day, while the best overflow-safe trigger, 98 items, costs USD 3.445 a day — marginally more than the schedule, the premium for holding the threshold far enough below capacity — with a mean wait of 1.87 days.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24121582

How Short Can a Location Code Be? Cell Size, Search Distance, and the Code Length a Settlement Actually Needs

The result. Many settlements have no street names, so a delivery address must be built from a grid code spoken, written on an envelope, or sent by phone. This paper computes how long such a code needs to be. A square settlement 4 km on a side is divided by a 20-symbol grid alphabet; each additional pair of characters divides the cell side by 20.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24121635

When to Reorder Mailbags and Forms: An Order Quantity and Reorder Point a Small Branch Can Actually Run

The result. A branch office does not run out of forms because it uses them; it runs out because nobody computed when to reorder. This paper applies the standard inventory results with stated local numbers. For printed forms used at 42.0 a day with a 12.0-day supplier lead time, the economic order quantity is 1256 units (12.2 orders a year, 29.9 days of cover each), and the reorder point is 565 units — 504 to cover the lead time plus 61 of safety stock for a 97.5 percent cycle service level.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24121649

One Clerk or Two at the Counter? The Arrival Rate at Which a Second Server Pays for Itself in Waiting Time

The result. A small post office counter is staffed by habit until the queue becomes a local scandal. This paper computes the staffing threshold from queueing theory: customers arrive at random, service averages four minutes, and the service standard is a mean queue wait of at most ten minutes. One clerk meets that standard only up to 10.7 arrivals per hour; at 12 arrivals an hour the mean wait with one clerk is 16.0 minutes against 0.76 minutes with two, and at 14 per hour it is 56.0 minutes against 1.11.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24121660

Relay Posts for Foot Couriers: How Long a Stage Can Be When the Courier Must Also Walk Home

The result. Where no vehicle goes, mail still moves by relay: couriers walk stages between posts, handing the bag onward like a baton. This paper sizes the stage from the courier's day. A courier walks loaded at a stated 3.6 km/h and returns unladen at 4.4 km/h; if the same courier must walk out and back within a seven-hour working day, the longest stage is 13.8 km.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24121673

Daily or Alternate-Day Delivery? The Letter Volume at Which Running the Round Every Day Stops Paying

The result. Halving delivery frequency is the first economy every struggling postal operator considers, and it is usually debated as ideology — service versus savings. This paper prices it. A round costs a stated USD 64 each day it runs; a letter-day of delay is priced at USD 0.05.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24121681

Drone or Porter on a Hill Path? A Distance Crossover for Light, Urgent Postal Loads

The result. For light urgent loads — medicines, documents, blood samples, a single critical parcel — hill settlements are served either by a porter on a path or, increasingly, by a small drone. This paper compares the two on stated costs: a porter paid USD 1.20 per hour walking at 3 km/h loaded and 4 km/h returning, against a drone cruising at 40 km/h whose capital, battery and maintenance costs are spread over eight trips a day. The drone becomes the cheaper mode per trip beyond a one-way distance of 6.5 km.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24121697

Mail by Boat: The Dispatch Interval That Balances Waiting Letters Against the Cost of the Trip

The result. For a settlement reached by boat, every dispatch decision trades a trip's cost against a pile of waiting letters. This paper prices both sides: a mail trip costs a stated USD 38, and a letter held one day carries a stated delay cost of USD 0.04. At 160 items a day the balance falls at a dispatch interval of 3.45 days, an average wait of 1.72 days, and a combined daily cost of USD 22.05 — 46.5 percent cheaper than dispatching daily (USD 41.2) and 20.8 percent cheaper than a weekly boat (USD 27.83).

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24121712

Sizing a Village Parcel Locker Bank: How Many Compartments Keep Turned-Away Parcels Under Two Percent

The result. A parcel locker bank fails quietly: it does not queue, it turns parcels away when every compartment is occupied. This paper sizes the bank as an Erlang loss system. Parcels arrive at 15 per day and stay a mean of 2.0 days awaiting collection, an offered load of 30.0 compartment-days per day.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24121720

How Long Should an Uncollected Parcel Wait? A Holding-Period Rule from the Decay of Collection

The result. Every parcel office holds a shelf of the uncollected, and every office has a rule — a week, a fortnight, a month — for when a parcel goes back to its sender. The rule is rarely derived. This paper derives it from the shape of collection itself: recipients collect with a probability that decays over days (modelled as a Weibull curve with a stated scale of 4.2 days), a stated 6.0 percent never collect at all, a shelf day costs USD 0.09, and a return costs USD 3.80.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24121736

How Many Digits Does a Local Postal Code Need? Capacity, Growth Headroom, and a Check Character That Catches Copying Errors

The result. A local postal code introduced for one settlement must do three jobs: name every household, leave room to grow, and betray its own copying errors. This paper sizes the code for a settlement of 8500 households with a stated growth factor of 1.8 over the planning horizon and a quarter of the code space held in reserve: 20400 codes are required, so 5 digits — capacity 100000, a headroom of 4.9 times — plus one check character, for a total length of 6. The check character, a weighted mod-11 sum, was verified by exhaustive testing rather than asserted: all 180000 single-digit errors in the test set were caught, and all 14410 adjacent transpositions were caught.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24121744

How Many Stops Fit in a Delivery Round? A Time Bound for a Single Rural Postman

The result. A delivery round is planned by drawing a boundary on a map, but what actually limits it is time. This paper bounds the number of stops a round can contain: from a 360-minute working round, fixed overhead takes 75 minutes, each stop takes 1.6 minutes of service, and travel between stops in a 6.0 km2 area follows the Beardwood-Halton-Hammersley square-root estimate at bicycle speed. The bound is 116 stops, using 98.9 minutes of travel and 185.6 minutes of service; one more stop would take the round to 361.6 minutes.

Paper · DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24121750

Sizing a Hand-Sorting Frame: When One Pass Stops Reaching, and What a Second Pass Costs in Errors

The result. Hand sorting looks like a furniture problem — how big a frame of pigeonholes to build — and is actually a decision about passes, reach and errors. A sorter can work comfortably with at most a stated 48 compartments within reach. An office sorting for 180 delivery rounds therefore cannot sort in one pass; the computed two-pass plan uses 13 first-pass area groups and 14 compartments in all, at a handling time of 135.0 minutes for 900 items.

Given, not sold

The knowledge itself is published, not held back. Exolex University, the company's school, is free and open to everyone. A personal Dave is free. And every design on this page is published openly, each with a DOI, for anyone in the world to read, check, and learn from. What is sold is the licence to manufacture — the record itself is given away.
